Hideo Kojima Is 'Inventing A New Medium' - Bill Skarsgård Shares Why Starring In Xbox's PHYSINT Is A 'Big Deal'

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

"I think Kojima is a pioneer, and keeps going out to untrodden waters."

Hideo Kojima always brings a certain sense of mystery and allure to his work, as is currently the case with the OD KNOCK and PHYSINT projects. For quite some time, speculation over who would star in the latter threw all sorts of crazy names into the mixer, before Kojima revealed that Pennywise and Dune actor Bill Skarsgård had got the job.

Skarsgård recently appeared on the Happy Sad Confused podcast, where he spoke about how working with Hideo Kojima is a "big deal" for him. He also admitted that he grew up playing all the Metal Gear Solid games and is a big fan of Kojima's process.

"I played every single one [Metal Gear Solid], at least the ones on platform. So, I was very enamoured by him and the sort of the mythology or the universes that he creates, but also the sort of mystique and allure about him as well."

As the actor continued to praise the Metal Gear creator, Skarsgård seemed to specifically be impressed by Kojima's fluent knowledge of filmmaking despite being a video game developer. He even recalled a moment where he asked Kojima if he would ever make a film:

"[Kojima] is an auteur, but he's a video game auteur. He's fluent in filmmaking, in film, and in cinema. I remember at one point, way back 20 years ago, there was talk about doing a Metal Gear Solid movie, and, is gonna Hideo gonna direct it? I asked him, 'would you ever do a film?' and he's like 'no'. At this point, what he's doing is inventing a new medium."

Skarsgård touched briefly on his performance capture in PHYSINT and suggested he's working on eight hours of narrative storytelling.

"What we're gonna do is eight hours worth of narrative, right? But it's also an entire universe that you play, so it truly is quite literally world building. But now with technology, we're getting closer and closer to something that's photorealistic, but it's also interactive when you play it. I think he's a pioneer, and keeps going out to untrodden waters."

PHYSINT is a return to tactical espionage for Kojima since departing Konami and Metal Gear Solid in 2015. We're very excited to see what he's been cooking up with Bill and the rest of the cast when we eventually learn more. Xbox has also remained fairly quiet on how PHYSINT fits into Microsoft's exclusivity plans.

Americans Bag Major First Ascent in Pakistan

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Americans Dane Steadman and Cody Winckler, awarded with a Piolet d’Or in 2025 after the first ascent of Yashkuk Sar, have done a similar monster 1,800m-long new route on the north face of Sosbun Brakk (6,414m). This time, Grant Stewart joined them for this off-season climb in the Karakoram.

This first ascent of the peak’s north face included difficulties up to M7, WI5. The climbers had to overcome several obstacles before even setting foot on the face. Like many other alpine-style teams this season, they were originally planning to climb another peak. But instead of adapting because of washed-out trails, they had bureaucratic issues. Their permit, obtained 10 months earlier, was revoked some weeks before their departure. They had to quickly find a different goal.

Meanwhile, the airline misplaced Winckler’s baggage — every adventurer’s nightmare. Somehow, the outfitter sent it to base camp some days later. Then, Steadman had to overcome serious Acute Mountain Sickness in base camp. Their acclimatization was hampered by freak spells of bad weather. Finally, the line on Sosbun Brakk they initially chose was too hard and risky because of a hanging serac they only spotted after they began.

The team’s lonely base camp. Photo: Dane Steadman

“Back at BC, it was time to rest, regroup, and contemplate a totally different line that we’d seen from on the wall, which gave us new hope,” Steadman recalled.

Off to the north face. Photo: Dane Steadman

On October 1, the three climbers set off on that potential new line. “Instead of angling left up hard mixed ground above the cone as before, we made an arcing traverse right, across an ice apron, to gain runnels on the right side of the face,” Steadman explained.

The team gained 800m on that first day and set their first bivouac on a snowy arete, just in time to be slammed by a violent storm that kept them in their tent the whole next day.

Steep mixed terrain. Photo: Dane Steadman

A tarp to protect the team’s bivouac from falling spindrift. Photo: Dane Steadman

“In the morning, we made a gamble for the top by leaving the tent and starting up a massive gully system,” Steward wrote. “At its top, we reached another dead end in a huge serac barrier. To escape this, Dane committed to a visionary pitch, casting off up a blank slab to eventually reach an ice smear.”

Chaotic, difficult rocky sections. Photo: Dane Steadman

“A huge serac to our right and smooth slabs out left posed a serious question; eight meters of sideways teetering on tiny gneissic edges and a miraculous rock thread were the answer,” said Steadman.

“We continued up the massive hanging glacier to reach the hallucinatory final headwall of steep ice/mixed climbing and mushroom-weaving lunacy,” Stewart continued. “One final traverse with 1,800m of exposure under our feet and a magically appearing, dead-vertical ice tongue led us to the summit ridge.”

A totally vertical section. Photo: Grant Stewart

“The wild terrain on the upper mountain took longer than we wanted,” Steadman admitted. By the time they finally reached the summit, it was 4 pm, the storm was coming the following day, and they still had a long way (and many rappels) down.

Summit picture on Sosbun Brakk. Left to right, Dane Steadman, Grant Stewart, and Cody Wrincker. Photo: Dane Steadman

“The sun would set around 6 pm, so we threaded the first of 35 rappels and started down. Long after dark, we finally arrived back at our bivy ledge at around 5,800m,” Steadman recalled.

The storm was not supposed to start until noon the following day, but it was already snowing at 8 am, with still 1,200 vertical meters of descent to go.

Rappelling down the route. Photo: Dane Steadman

“Before long, we were caught in a proper storm,” Stewart explained. “We committed to a faster and hopefully safer descent by rappelling down the west rib to the Sokha/Sosbun col. By the time we reached the col, we could barely communicate over the screaming southwest wind.”

After some scary rappels in high winds down the serac band, the climbers finally reached a sheltered section of the face. They still had to deal with heavy spindrift coming from above until finally, at around 4 pm, they touched down on the glacier. And yet, the adventure was not over — especially for Grant Stewart:

Following Dane, I took maybe 10 steps when I felt the ground under me crack. Instinctively, I jumped and landed on my stomach as a huge crevasse swallowed the ground behind me. Soaking wet, we began postholing into the wan light of a mesmerizing white out.

[We stuck] to the center of the glacier as avalanches rumbled down all around in the whirling snow. After a strange eternity, the ragged colors of a tent appeared and we were greeted by ecstatic dancing, big hugs, and endless cups of chai from (our BC crew) Ibrahim and Idrees.

Happy climbers and base camp crew after a safe return from the summit. Photo: Dane Steadman

The team didn’t report their success and the details of the climb until they returned home. And then, the first words of appraisal were not for the route itself, but for the mountains and people of Gilgit-Baltistan.

“It’s a unique place…where continents and cultures collide. Trips there, while often difficult, are rewarding in ways hard to describe simply,” Steadman said. This was his third trip to the Karakoram, a place he has fallen in love with.

Angela Benavides graduated university in journalism and specializes in high-altitude mountaineering and expedition news. She has been writing about climbing and mountaineering, adventure and outdoor sports for 20+ years.

Prior to that, Angela Benavides spent time at/worked at a number of local and international media. She is also experienced in outdoor-sport consultancy for sponsoring corporations, press manager and communication executive, and a published author.

ExplorersWeb is part of AllGear Digital's portfolio of media brands.

Fact check: Three viral Iran war posts debunked

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

In the wake of the new US attack on Iran, social media is awash with dramatic images and claims about the fighting. DW Fact Check found AI-generated and recycled videos, as well as misleading posts from dubious accounts.

"The reason we lost is the war," US Vice President JD Vance said, shortly after Republicans suffered major losses during the US midterm elections in both the House and Senate. He was referring to the US-Israel war against Iran.

Hours before the US midterms, the US reportedly struck Iran's Qeshm Island, despite Trump previously saying that there would be no new strikes before the elections.

Among fears of a renewed regional escalation, social media has been flooded with dramatic images and purported battlefield updates. Not all of them are genuine.

DW Fact check takes a closer look at three viral claims.

Claim: In this post on X, a user claims that Iran launched a massive missile and drone attack on a US aircraft carrier, resulting in fatalities. It is accompanied by a video showing an explosion and its aftermath on what appears to be an aircraft carrier. The post has garnered over 643.000 views.

The 15-second video is an AI-generated fake.

Although there have been reports of Iranian forces attempting to shoot down a US aircraft, there have been no credible reports of a successful attack on a US aircraft carrier which resulted in fatalities.

The AI video also predates the latest US strikes — it has circulated at least since May this year. At that time, it was shared alongside a different claim unrelated to the current situation.

Frame-by-frame analysis reveals several inconsistencies. At approximately three seconds in, the feet of a running person visually glitch. The sea in the background appears uniform and almost video game-like. At five seconds, several people appear to pass through one another, and their limbs also glitch.

The voices in the audio sound synthetic and appear closely synchronized with the visuals. Both AI Detection tools by Meta AI and Google Gemini identified strong indicators that the video was AI-generated. It is important to note, however, that such tools provide an assessment of likelihood, rather than definitive proof that something was created using AI.

The video is considerably shaky and short, two characteristics that warrant closer scrutiny of possible AI use.

The visual nature of supposedly chaotically filmed videos can cover up glitches that still happen frequently in AI-generated content.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Claim: This user on X shares a video with the claim that "Iran attacks the US bases in the Gulf." A burning building is visible with large dark plumes of smoke rising from it. It has been viewed over 55.000 times.

A reverse image search shows that the images are recycled and show a fire in South Korea in 2025.

News reports indicated that a fire broke out at a Fashion Logistics Center in Cheonan. The same building is visible in the street-level view of South Korea’s Naver Maps street imagery, and its location matches the red building in a mountainous area which is shown on Google Maps satellite imagery.

Visual cues that match include the building itself, the white lettering "E-Land fashion" on the right side and the mountains in the background.

The reverse image search also reveals that the video has repeatedly circulated. It made rounds in July this year attached to Iran war posts in Tamil and English with one post on X reaching 1.4 million views. Other posts linked it  to the war in Yemenand other conflicts, unrelated to the fire in South Korea.

Claim: "Iran has launched a major hypersonic missile attack targeting multiple Israeli military bases," an X account  writes. By the time of writing, the post had 2.5 million views.

Iran claims to have  hypersonic missiles and has previously claimed to have fired Fattah-1 missiles at Israel, most recently in June 2025.  There are no credible reports about Iranian hypersonic missile attacks during the latest escalation.

The account that has published the claim has over 100.000 followers and posts with high frequency, often over ten times a day. Most of the posts reach hundred thousands of views, often using urgent "Breaking" headlines and repeating similar posts. In one post for example, it claims "the war has officially started" reaching more than 30 million views.  These are all hallmarks of a so-called "content farm."

In a comment to DW, analyst Charlene Lin from the internet watchdog NewsGuard writes that this account is a South-Asia based content farm. The account has already been flagged in relation to other disinformation narratives.

Lin writes that such South Asia-based content farms "seem to spread false claims about geopolitics by typically posing as pro-China/Russia/Iran users."

She adds that the account amplifies false claims alongside other accounts that appear to be part of the same network, typically within a short period of time.

Victoire Rio, an expert on social-media monetization and information integrity, wrote to DW that "it's pretty common to see financially-motivated actors from South and Southeast Asia leverage prominent news issues to generate a financial profit off the engagement on platforms like X."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

OUTFRONT Media (OUT) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Revenue climbed on transit strength and a World Cup boost, with leverage easing.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

OUTFRONT Media Inc. (OUT -8.73%) reported a 9.3% increase in total revenues to $511.1 million for the third quarter, driven by double-digit growth in the transit segment and contributions from the FIFA World Cup. Management indicated that the company is reinvesting savings from its 2025 restructuring into digital capabilities, AI tools, and sales enablement to capture a larger share of programmatic advertising spend. The company raised its full-year AFFO growth guidance to approximately 25% based on current revenue momentum and disciplined execution against strategic imperatives. Management stated that the physical advertising layer remains a critical trust foundation for brands as consumer skepticism toward online, AI-generated content continues to rise.

Operator: I will now hand the conference over to Stephan Bisson, SVP of Investor Relations. Stephan, please go ahead.

Stephan Bisson: Good afternoon, and thank you for joining our 2026 third quarter earnings call. With me on the call today are CEO Nick Brien and CFO Matthew Siegel. After a discussion of our financial results, we will open the lines for a question-and-answer session. Our comments today will refer to the earnings release and slide presentation that you can find on the Investor Relations section of our website, outfront.com. After today's call has concluded, an audio archive replay will be available there as well. This conference call may include forward-looking statements.

Relevant factors that could cause actual results to differ materially from these forward-looking statements are listed in our earnings materials and in our SEC filings, including our 2025 Form 10-K, as well as our Q3 2026 Form 10-Q, which we expect to file tomorrow. We will refer to certain non-GAAP financial measures on this call. Any references to OIBDA made today will be on an adjusted basis. Reconciliations of OIBDA and other non-GAAP financial measures are in the appendix of the slide presentation, the earnings release, and on our website, which also includes presentations with prior period reconciliations. With that, let me hand the call over to Nick.

Nick Brien: Thanks, Stephan, and thank you everyone for joining us today. We are excited to report our positive third quarter results. Revenue came in better than we had anticipated when we spoke in August, given continued strong demand for our premium IRL media assets, a focused and motivated sales force, and the second portion of our successful FIFA World Cup, which generated about $15 million during the third quarter, and about $50 million overall. As you can see on slide three, which summarizes our headline numbers, consolidated revenues were up 9%, driven by 20% growth in transit and 6% growth in billboard, while consolidated OIBDA was up 6% to $145 million, and AFFO grew 5% to $103 million.

As I just mentioned, these results include FIFA revenues, of which we believe approximately half are incremental to our usual business. Slide four shows our more detailed revenue results. Billboard revenues were up 5.9%, driven by strong, high single-digit growth in our East region. The strongest billboard categories in Q3 were tech, fueled by continued AI momentum, legal, and health/medical. Transit grew 20% during the quarter and was again led by the New York MTA, which was up 22% in Q3. Our strongest transit categories were tech, entertainment, and retail. Hopefully many of you have seen the AI company's significant presence in the New York City subways. Slide five shows our detailed billboard revenue.

Digital billboard revenues were up 12.8%, and static and other billboard revenues were up 2.8% during the quarter. We estimate that FIFA contributed approximately $8 million of the revenue to our billboard results this quarter. Slide six shows our detailed transit revenue, which grew over 20% during the quarter, led by continued strength within the New York MTA. Our digital transit revenues were up nearly 17% to about $65 million, and static transit revenues were up over 24%, given an increase in demand for station dominations. We estimate that FIFA contributed approximately $6 million to our transit revenues in the third quarter.

Slide seven shows our combined digital revenue performance, which grew over 14% in the third quarter and now represents about 37% of total revenues compared to 35% last year. Programmatic and digital direct automated sales increased nearly 16% during the quarter, representing nearly 20% of total digital revenues. Moving on, the breakdown of commercial and enterprise revenues can be seen on slide eight. Commercial revenues were up 12% during the quarter, driven by strength in legal, tech, and entertainment. Enterprise was up 6% during the third quarter, with much of the strength being driven by tech, retail, and CPG.

Slide nine shows our billboard yield growth, which was up 9% year-over-year to $3,318 per month, principally driven by a focused effort to realize increased value across our premium assets, as well as the continued success of FIFA spending. Summing up, we are quite pleased with our Q3 performance and our bookings thus far in the fourth quarter, which we believe will allow us to deliver double-digit revenue growth for the full year. With that, let me now hand it over to Matt, who will review the rest of our financials.

Matthew Siegel: Thanks, Nick, and good afternoon, everyone. Please turn to slide 10 for a more detailed look at our billboard expenses. In total, billboard expenses were up nearly $21 million, or approximately 10% year-over-year. Focusing in on lease costs, these expenses were up $7 million, or about 7% year-over-year. This increase was driven by both higher variable lease costs and contractual escalators on fixed leases. Posting, maintenance, and other, or PMO expenses, decreased slightly during the quarter as lower production expenses were partially offset by higher site-related costs and higher compensation-related expenses.

SG&A expenses grew approximately $13 million, primarily driven by higher professional fees, including software and technology expenses, where we reinvested a portion of the savings generated by our 2025 RIF into improving our sales enablement capabilities, and also higher compensation-related expenses and higher allowance for bad debt. The $21 million increase in total billboard expenses was offset by the strong growth in billboard revenues Nick described earlier, leading to billboard Adjusted OIBDA to increase slightly during the quarter. Now, turning to transit on slide 11. In total, transit expenses were up nearly $10 million, or about 10% year-over-year.

Transit franchise expense was up 8% to $65 million, due primarily to higher variable transit franchise expenses, driven by higher transit revenues outside New York and the annual inflation adjustment to the MAG for the MTA contract. Transit PMO costs were up less than half a million dollars, or about 2%. SG&A expenses were up $4.6 million, due primarily to higher professional fees, including software and technology expenses, higher compensation-related expenses, including commissions, a higher allowance for bad debt, and higher credit card usage by customers, similar to our billboard SG&A variance.

The nearly $10 million increase in total transit expenses was eclipsed by our exceptional 20% transit revenue growth described earlier, leading to transit Adjusted OIBDA increasing by over $13 million during the quarter to $29 million. Slide 12 shows the company's Adjusted OIBDA in the third quarter. Corporate expense increased by about $5 million, due primarily to higher compensation-related expenses and professional fees, partially offset by the impact of market fluctuations on an unfunded equity-linked retirement plan offered by the company to certain employees. The professional fees included a top-tier management consultant focused on modernizing our business practices and improving operational speed and cost efficiency.

Combined with the billboard and transit OIBDA, total consolidated Adjusted OIBDA totaled about $145 million, up 6% compared to last year. Before moving on, I'd like to discuss our recent expense trends and our expectations going forward. First, as we mentioned on our last earnings call, given our robust revenue performance and strong outlook for the year, we are investing proactively for continued future growth. We are strengthening all of our digital capabilities for enhanced growth, our ad tech, our sales teams, our data analytics, and our inventory management. We continue to invest significantly to strengthen our sales force with additional training and advanced tools.

We have also invested in our HR function to ensure that we recruit, retain, and develop the best possible talent from across the media industry. Lastly, we are also investing in AI enablement. During 2026, we developed and internally released Launchpad, our proprietary AI workspace, across our entire organization. It gives our teams direct access to our inventory, audience, and market data, along with mapping and proposal tools, so they spend less time gathering information and more time prospecting for new business and growing our existing clients. More than half of our commercial sales team now use it every month.

We also began rolling out IRL NAV, our new audience-based planning platform, and our full sales team moves onto it early next year. Also in 2027, we plan to open IRL NAV to small and local advertisers so they can enjoy the speed and simplicity of self-service media management. Second, in addition to these investments, our growth in SG&A and corporate expenses also reflect higher incentive compensation due to our exceptional financial performance this year, driven by our tremendous top-line growth.

Specifically, in the third quarter, approximately half of the increase in our SG&A and corporate expenses were due to success-based variable compensation and payments, which were earned in the form of higher commissions, incentive bonuses, and professional fees, as we are significantly outperforming our budget this year. Looking forward to the fourth quarter, we expect SG&A and corporate expenses combined to grow in the high teens compared to 2025. As with the case in the third quarter, we expect that over half of the increase will be related to success-based expenses. We view these bonuses as evidence of our thriving 2026. Turning now to capital expenditures on slide 13.

Q3 CapEx spend was about $19 million, including about $7 million on maintenance spend. We added 29 new digital billboards in Q3 and expect to add a total of about 125 in the full year. For 2026, we now expect to spend approximately $85 million of CapEx, with $30 million-$35 million of this total being maintenance-related. Looking at AFFO on slide 14, you can see the bridge to our Q3 AFFO of $103 million. The improvement is principally driven by higher adjusted OIBDA, offset by slightly higher cash taxes, maintenance CapEx, and other items.

Based on our results thus far, our expected revenue growth for Q4, and the ongoing investments in our business, we now expect that our reported 2026 AFFO will grow around 25% compared to our reported 2025 AFFO of $338 million. Included in this raised guidance is the maintenance CapEx I just mentioned, interest expense of just over $140 million, and a small amount of cash taxes. Please turn to slide 15 for an update on our balance sheet. Committed liquidity is over $600 million, including about $75 million of cash, around $500 million available via our revolver, and $50 million available via our accounts receivable securitization facility.

As of September 30th, our total net leverage was 3.9 times below our four to five times target range. Turning to our dividend, we announced today that our board of directors maintained the $0.33 cash dividend payable on December 31st to stockholders of record at the close of business on December 4th. We spent just under $3 million on acquisitions during the quarter, and looking at our current acquisition pipeline, we continue to expect our 2026 full-year deal activity to be a little higher than last year. With our leverage trending below our target range and increasing cash flows, we expect to be more active in the acquisition space going forward.

With that, let me turn the call back to Nick.

Nick Brien: Thank you, Matt. Our revenue momentum is strong, and it's accelerating into Q4. We expect Q4 revenue growth to reach double digits, driven by our over 20% growth in transit and mid-single-digit growth in billboard. This is no accident. It's a result of disciplined execution against our 2026 strategic imperatives, sharper focus on our priority growth opportunities, and a stronger AI-enabled sales engine. on the last call, I told you we'd commissioned Kantar to study consumer trust across media in the U.S. The research is now published, and the findings validate our strong belief that IRL media has a much bigger role to play in the future of brand advertising, and that Outfront is well-positioned to lead it.

Four key findings stand out. First, trust online is breaking down. 93% of consumers surveyed now question whether the content they see online was created by a person or generated by AI. Social media ads were rated the most likely of any format to be fake, misleading, or AI-generated. Gen Z and millennials are the most skeptical of any group. Second, reality is becoming more valuable. 78% of consumers are seeking out real-world experiences more than they did last year. Gen Z and millennials, the consumers our clients most want to reach, are leading the shift. The generation raised by algorithms values connection, surprise, discovery, and real-world experiences. The future of media isn't less technology, it's more reality.

Third, the physical layer creates the trust foundation. Out-of-home is consistently perceived as the most trusted real-life advertising channel, and the least likely to be fake or misleading by every generation surveyed. When consumers saw the identical ad on social media and on a billboard, they rated the billboard version at least twice as credible, believable, and trustworthy. Fourth, the role of media in the AI era is changing quickly. Reach and targeting still matter, but the media environment a brand chooses now either strengthens or weakens its trust and credibility. As AI continues to flood the internet, the physical layer becomes more essential to brands.

It makes every campaign more credible and carries that credibility across the entire media plan. Our assets live in that layer, and what we offer advertisers has never been more valuable. As Matt described earlier, that's why we're continuing to invest in the talent, technology, measurement, and AI tools across every function to unlock this growth opportunity and accelerate profitability. Trupanion (TRUP) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Record profit came with a warning on veterinary cost inflation.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Trupanion, Inc. (TRUP +1.93%) reported record adjusted operating income and free cash flow for the third quarter of 2026. Management reported that the subscription business drove the majority of top-line growth, supported by a seventh consecutive quarter of improved retention and a 28% increase in net new pet enrollments. The company continues to invest in technology and automation, which has resulted in over 65% of claims being processed automatically. Strategic initiatives, including a new digital product scheduled for a mid-2027 launch and limited trials for the Landspath pet food line, remain primary focal points for long-term expansion. Additionally, the company initiated share repurchases following a $100 million authorization and reduced its outstanding debt through disciplined capital allocation.

Our long standing investments in technology, automation, and AI continue to pay off across the business.

And it starts with conversion. We have continued to refine our online enrollment journey, making it easier for pet parents to understand our product and its unique differences and to enroll with confidence. Those improvements, coupled with the continued rollout of expanded deductible and coinsurance options, now implemented entirely across Canada and roughly 90% of the US, contributed to a strengthened web conversion rate in the third quarter. Retention improved for the seventh consecutive quarter, also supported by the aforementioned product updates. These increased variability options on our leading products are beginning to unlock access to more consumers. While helping to deepen pet parents' understanding of the lifetime coverage that we offer.

Once the pet parent becomes a member, our patented software lets us pay veterinarians directly at checkout. No other provider in North America can do this. In Q3, we added the most veterinary clinics using our software in a quarter in nearly 5 years. Behind the scenes, claims automation has climbed from under 15 percent in 2020. To over 60-5 percent today. Our proprietary software, along with claims automation, enables us to approve a claim and pay a veterinarian in a matter of seconds, and it provides us with lower processing costs to drive operating leverage, and enhanced experience for our members.

Combined with our long standing veterinary relationships built over the course of many years, this technology gives Trupanion a durable moat that is hard to replicate. Importantly, our investments in technology automation, and AI are not meant to replace the human touch. But rather protect it. When routing claims are processed automatically and tools like Scout, our AI virtual agent, answer everyday questions, our teams have more time for the moments that matter most. The pet parent facing a difficult diagnosis, the complex claim that needs careful judgment, or the veterinary team that needs a quick answer at checkout. Good pet insurance is ultimately about trust. And trust is built by people.

Technology lets us deliver an experience that is simpler, faster, and more personal so that when a member reaches out to us, they reach someone who can focus entirely on supporting them not navigating friction. Turning to the new digital offering, our next generation product designed to grow the market, Since our last call, the work has progressed well, and we remain on track to launch by the mid point of our strategic plan next summer. Our coinsurance and deductible rollout has been instructive. it is confirmed meaningful demand from pet parents who want Trupanions protection at a more accessible starting point. And that learning is directly shaping how we approach branding, and pricing in the new product.

I am pleased to report that we have begun limited trials, which is an important milestone for us as we move closer to launch in 2027. As a reminder, Landspath is a long term opportunity into which we will continue to invest at a measured pace. We believe a portion control food sold directly through veterinarians can improve pet health deepen member loyalty, and return revenue to our veterinary partners over time. While Landspath broadens the way we can support pet health and strengthen our veterinary relationships, raising awareness of pet insurance is our most immediate focus.

And driving general category understanding remains central to unlocking a large and under market. There are more than 180 million pets across The United States and Canada. Yet only a little over 7 million pet parents have insurance. We estimate that more than 1 million new pets are added to veterinary practice management systems every month. Each represents the pet parent making decisions about their pet's care. And an opportunity for us to be part of that conversation. In October, we announced a new partnership with television personality and music executive Simon Cowell. Simon has built a career and a reputation on recognizing quality. And he brought that same discerning eye to pet insurance.

He came to us with hard questions. Did his own homework, and chose Trupanion for his own 4 dogs. Now he is helping pet parents understand what great pet insurance looks like. No payout limits, coverage that keeps pace with modern veterinary medicine, and a veterinarian is paid directly at checkout. When Simon speaks, people listen. Mortgage Rates Today, Friday, November 6: A Little Lower

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

TL;DR: Mortgage rates dropped today, but inflation data could shake things up next week.

With the midterm elections behind us, mortgage rates are continuing their gradual slide this month. One factor is a sell-off in tech stocks, which can send investors toward the relative safety of bonds and help push mortgage rates lower.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage ticked down to 6.96% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is five basis points lower than yesterday and 11 basis points lower than a week ago. (See our chart below for more specifics.) A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.

Meanwhile, a stronger-than-expected jobs report could slow that slide by reinforcing expectations for another Federal Reserve rate hike in December.

While the economy never sleeps, markets are closed on the weekends. The rates you see Friday are unlikely to change much (if at all) until Monday.

As for what’s next: Tomorrow’s jobs report, showing October data, could potentially nudge mortgage rates in either direction. To understand the link between jobs data and mortgage rates, keep reading below the video.

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

The recent slide in mortgage rates might reverse course if next week’s inflation data comes in hot.

This week, the Nerds are watching the October Consumer Price Index, which comes out on Tuesday, Nov. 10. CPI is one of two key measures of inflation, which the Federal Reserve aims to keep in check relative to the strength of the labor market. (The Fed looks more closely at the other data set, the Personal Consumption Expenditures Index, which comes out Nov. 25.)

Right now, the labor market is looking pretty darn healthy: The October jobs report, released today from the Bureau of Labor Statistics, showed U.S. employers added 165,000 jobs in October, far more than economists expected, and unemployment edged down to 4.1%.

Raising the federal funds rate is the Fed’s main tool for bringing inflation down, but rate hikes are only on the table when the labor market is strong enough to handle it. Right now, it’s looking fairly likely that the Fed will approve a rate hike when the committee meets Dec. 8-9. The Fed doesn't set mortgage rates directly, but an anticipated rate hike can put upward pressure on mortgage rates, which respond to investors’ expectations for the economy and Fed policy.

Still, a jump in mortgage rates isn’t a sure thing. October’s inflation data will capture the effect of the Fed’s last rate hike, which came in the middle of September. If that worked as expected and inflation shows signs of slowing, mortgage rates will be less affected.

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.46% or higher — but there aren't too many people for whom that's true.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

» Stay informed: Check out NerdWallet's mortgage news hub for all our latest coverage.

The rate you see advertised is a sample rate — usually for a borrower with perfect credit, making a big down payment, and paying for mortgage points. That won't match every buyer's circumstances.

It's a weird time to buy a laptop, but the new MacBook Pro doesn't skimp on quality

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

It has the best laptop screen we’ve used, but there may be reasons to wait.

Apple’s new MacBook Pro is expensive as hell.

Let’s get that out there right away, because for a lot of people, it will be the only thing that matters. As with so many things in life, though, you get what you pay for. There’s no questioning the quality of the laptop’s display or most of its other hardware components.

But should you buy it? Well, that’s a complicated question in 2026.

We’re in a time of great uncertainty when it comes to buying a computer. Prices are at levels we’ve not seen in recent history, and they may well keep climbing. Meanwhile, users paying close attention to the early developments in both local and agentic artificial intelligence are asking an important question: “What will computing even look like in a few years, and what does that mean for the computer I should buy today?”

Apple’s new MacBook Pro sits at the intersection of all of this uncertainty. In multiple ways, it’s also straddling different worlds.

It arrives amid a changing of the guard in Apple’s leadership, potentially raising questions about the future direction of Apple’s ecosystem. It’s a mass-market consumer product, but configuring it with enough RAM and storage to make it feel future-proof pushes its price into territory mostly only well-funded businesses can afford. It’s firmly rooted in the computing paradigms of the past 20 years, but it shows early signs of flirtation with new AI workflows. It’s a conventional Apple laptop in most ways, but it also has a touchscreen—something that would never have happened in a prior Apple era.

It’s an outstanding, high-quality product, but whether it’s something you should buy depends on you—what you’re doing right now, what you’ll be doing in the future, and what you value.

For most of the specs we look at, Apple isn’t claiming any difference here. The new MacBook Pro promises 24 hours of battery life (though you’d have to use it in a conservative way to achieve that), it has the same ports (three Thunderbolt, one HDMI, one SDXC, and MagSafe), and it has the N1 chip for Wi-Fi 7 and Bluetooth 6 connectivity.

And like its immediate predecessor, it offers the M5 Pro and M5 Max chips as options. Running synthetic benchmarks, we found no meaningful difference at all compared to when we ran the same tests on the previous model, so we won’t spend time discussing their performance today. We’ve covered both thoroughly in prior reviews, and you can read up there if you’d like.

The chips are more than fast enough for normal workflows for the foreseeable future, including most 3D work. AI inference is the main area where they may be less future-proof. To be clear, they’re strong enough for now, but the field is evolving rapidly, and there’s reason to expect significant improvements in future chips on this front.

According to Bloomberg, Apple is skipping Pro and Max versions of the M6 chip that was introduced earlier this year to focus its attention on a significant rework for the M7 due in 2027. “The M7 line is designed primarily around major advancements to on-device AI processing,” Bloomberg’s Mark Gurman wrote. The line would emphasize memory bandwidth, which matters for fast, efficient AI inference.

Even without Bloomberg’s report, I’d expect Apple to prioritize those areas in its next high-end chip refresh. That’s partly because there’s another tea leaf to read here: Apple made just such an update with the A20 Pro, which debuted in its latest iPhones several weeks ago. That update brought a substantially improved Neural Engine and a significant increase in memory bandwidth. We also found that the A20 Pro delivered improved 3D graphics performance in some cases.

Similar improvements could come to Macs, meaning it might be worth waiting another year if you’re focused on AI workflows or even 3D applications—especially given the high cost of these machines. That’s part of what makes this such an uncertain time to buy a laptop. We’re in a moment when these companies are revisiting the architecture of these chips because of these new workflows, and we don’t know what the end result is yet.

Here’s one thing that’s not at all uncertain: The display on the MacBook Pro is the best I have ever used in a laptop. If the display is your primary concern, this is a big moment.

Apple calls it a “tandem OLED display,” meaning a panel with two emissive layers. This approach helps achieve a higher peak brightness, but perhaps more importantly, it can reduce the risk of burn-in. Most of the time, the display won’t need both layers running at full power. Instead, they can share the load and run at a reduced current, helping extend the panel’s lifespan.

The new display can achieve up to 2,000 nits of peak brightness on HDR highlights, but it’s rated for 1,000 nits of sustained brightness. In general, I like the way Apple handles HDR for this at the OS level, too.

The brightness matters for HDR content, sure, but what really makes OLED stand out is per-pixel illumination—meaning each pixel can turn on or off independently. That allows for absolute black levels and very strong contrast. The MiniLED display in the previous MacBook Pro offered good contrast for an LCD display, but OLED has a fundamental advantage here, and it shows.

As you’d expect, the screen offers native support for both P3 and Adobe RGB color gamuts. It also has a variable refresh rate from 10 to 120 Hz, just like many of Apple’s other products. As before, the MacBook Pro comes in roughly 14-inch (2,024 by 1,986 pixels) and 16-inch (3,472 by 2,226 pixels) variants. There’s also a nano-texture glass option to fight glare, though that will set you back another $150.

It’s one of the most impressive displays I’ve used in any device, and it’s certainly the best I’ve ever used on a laptop. The colors are vibrant, the contrast is outstanding, the blacks are inky, and it can get very bright when it needs to—not just with highlights but full-screen brightness as well. And on the whole, it’s extremely precise. Uniformity appears basically flawless. It’s what you’d expect from an ultra-high-end TV or reference monitor, not a laptop or computer screen, and it’s genuinely striking.

We’ll see about longevity, but OLED has come a long way in recent years, and my first impressions are that Apple has made all the right choices here.

I find myself trying to find something negative to say about the MacBook Pro’s screen to avoid sounding too effusive, but I can’t think of anything substantive. The best I can do is complain that Apple’s proprietary “PROLED XDR” label is silly and eye-roll-inducing.

Branding aside, the screen is the main reason to pay extra for this model.

With its lid closed, the new MacBook Pro looks identical to its predecessor. The dimensions are largely the same. It’s slightly thinner, but you’d have to check the spec sheet or handle them side by side to notice.

Pick it up, though, and you’ll find that it’s noticeably lighter than the last model, by about half a pound. It’s not in a totally different weight class, but you can notice the change, and it’s a nice improvement. The reduction is partly due to the display technology switch; it’s easier to make a light OLED screen than a light MiniLED one.

Open the MacBook Pro and turn it on, and you’ll see one of a few other notable differences, depending on whether you’re behind or in front of it. From behind, you’ll see the Apple logo on the lid now lights up, as it did on MacBooks of yore. It’s arguably a little tacky, but it’s also neat and nostalgic. I don’t hate it.

From the front, you might notice that the already barely there bezels are even more barely there, though that’s subtle. More likely, you’ll see that the top-of-screen camera notch has been replaced by a cutout just like the Dynamic Island Apple introduced on the iPhone.

When I reviewed the first iPhone with a Dynamic Island, I was largely positive about it. No, it’s not ideal to have a cutout on the screen, but Apple has handled it as intelligently as possible, and I would even go so far as to say it has turned what should be an albatross into an attractive feature.

Thanks to the OLED display, it’s possible to make any of the pixels around the camera cutout just as black as it is. Apple has designed live activities, the volume bar, and more to appear as if they are expanding out of and around, and contracting back into, this little dot. It remains ingenious.

When the Dynamic Island first arrived on the iPhone, it helped unify several previously disparate approaches to displaying notifications in iOS, making it especially useful. This time, it doesn’t solve as many problems, but it’s an improvement over the dead weight of the notch. It also helps that the MacBook Pro’s much larger screen means the cutout takes up a small share of the screen’s real estate.

The Dynamic Island works the same with full-screen apps as the notch did. By default, the top of the viewport ends just below the Dynamic Island, so it just looks like there’s a taller top bezel, and it doesn’t cut into the application interface. The menu bar, clock, and other system elements still occupy the space above the application (set against a totally black background) when you move the cursor up there.

I tested a few fullscreen games with it, like World of Warcraft and Cyberpunk 2077. Some behaved by default like the default fullscreen app behavior I just described, but others tried to occupy the whole thing, making the cutout visible. Vitally, every video app I tried avoided the cutout in fullscreen.

This isn’t something to upgrade for, of course. But on the whole, I think it’s an improvement over what we had before.

Apple has finally added a touchscreen to the MacBook Pro. I say “finally” because the possibility has floated around for about two decades. Steve Jobs was famously opposed to the idea; there’s even video of him explaining why he thought it would make for a poor user experience.

A couple of years ago, reliable reports emerged that Apple was moving forward with the idea regardless. When I first heard about it, I imagined what could be a really exciting implementation: a convertible laptop that would let you use it in a tablet-like mode, with Apple Pencil support for drawing and writing.

As a game developer, I imagined quickly sketching 2D game maps with an Apple Pencil, using a local AI model to convert them into 3D geometry, and then switching to a laptop configuration to start testing and coding. I also imagined using it like an iPad to more comfortably watch movies on a flight.

That’s not what we got, though. The laptop has the same form factor as the previous MacBook Pro model—no tablet mode or Pencil support. Instead, it just lets you reach up from the keyboard and trackpad to touch the screen. That feels like a missed opportunity.

Some creative apps have implemented touch features that might justify the touchscreen functionality if you use them a lot. For example, you can play the on-screen virtual piano keys on Logic Pro with multiple fingers, which could be nicer than clicking on them. I imagine many people already have workflows built around other approaches, though.

If you were specifically waiting for a touchscreen on your MacBook Pro, this seems like as good an implementation as you could hope for without turning the laptop into a convertible. Apple has put real care into it. For example, it has re-engineered the hinge so the screen is not particularly wobbly when you touch it. It’s fast, accurate, and responsive. It supports multi-touch gestures like pinch-to-zoom or swiping with three fingers to switch desktop spaces.

There’s one use case I find compelling as a developer. Testing iPhone and iPad apps in the Simulator has always been a minor hassle because of the difference in input methods. You either have to approximate only a subset of the interactions available to users with a trackpad and mouse or connect an iPhone or iPad to test them that way. A touchscreen could provide a more elegant way to test such apps in Xcode, Unity, or whatever else you might use.

Unfortunately, some of the UI elements in macOS are too small for my large fingers, so I have to tap very carefully. I also keep forgetting the touchscreen is even there for hours at a time and go back to my normal habits, partly because there’s nothing you can do at the OS level that you can’t do just as easily with trackpad gestures. And as Jobs predicted all those years ago, my arm got tired while I was using it.

For me, it feels faster, easier, more comfortable, and more natural to use the keyboard and trackpad 100 percent of the time, but I admittedly have decades of muscle memory and habits built around them. There’s apparently a generation coming up with the same kind of familiarity with touch interfaces, so this feature may appeal to them. Plus, touchscreen laptops have been a thing on Windows forever, and some people seem to want to buy them, though I never quite got why without an added tablet configuration.

It’s unlikely to account for a big portion of the device’s cost compared with the rest of the OLED panel, memory, storage, and so on. So, my own bafflement aside, I’m not unhappy it’s here. I imagine it will serve those who want it well enough.

As noted earlier, whether this laptop is worthy of a recommendation depends on you and how you use your machine. That’s always been the case, but it seems more so here.

If you’re a creative professional or a display enthusiast who wants the best laptop screen you’ve likely ever seen, you won’t be disappointed if you shell out for it. And hey, maybe you can show me your workflow and prove me wrong about the touchscreen!

If you’re just looking for a great laptop with strong performance for everyday use, consider the non-OLED, non-touchscreen model that’s still in Apple’s lineup—or even a MacBook Air, which compromises relatively little for most users. And there is of course the entire world of Windows and Linux laptops.

If you’re a developer—especially one working at the frontier of AI workflows—well, that’s where things get tricky. Will waiting be worthwhile? So much about how those workflows will evolve and what they’ll demand from your hardware is still so up-in-the air that I can’t make a confident recommendation. (For what it’s worth, I fit best in this category, and I’m still holding on to my M1 Max machine, though my resolve is wavering.)

All that said, in most ways, this is still the same MacBook Pro we’ve reviewed again and again for the past few years. Touch adds a feature aimed at a particular niche, or perhaps it’s there to roll out the welcome mat for a newer generation. The display is a more substantial update and makes a compelling case for upgrading, especially if you’re coming from an older, pre-MiniLED laptop.

But this is an expensive machine. The configuration I reviewed here (16-inch, M5 Max, 128 GB of RAM, 2 TB SSD) retails for a whopping $7,399, though you could at least shave $1,600 off that by going to a more typical-for-most-people 64 GB. (The cheapest configuration starts at $2,899.)

For the vast majority of buyers, the pre-OLED version that’s still in Apple’s lineup or another MacBook altogether will offer more bang for your buck. If you’re the kind of person who wants OLED or a touchscreen, though, you probably know that already. Based on my time with the new MacBook Pro, both features deliver on their promises, even if the latter isn’t as ambitious as some of us had hoped.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Data centers don’t need special treatment to get power faster

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Projects that make their demand more predictable, controllable and financially secure should have a clearer path to service, writes Seyed Madaeni, CEO and co-founder of Verse.

Seyed Madaeni is CEO and co-founder of Verse, an energy software company specializing in battery dispatch optimization for data centers.

Even the best-funded project can’t operate without electricity, and the grid often can’t deliver hundreds of new megawatts as quickly as AI developers need them. Access to power has become a competitive advantage, and companies are searching for ways to get it faster.

Today, developers see two obvious options. They can wait years for traditional grid upgrades, or they can build private power plants onsite. The first can move too slowly. The second brings fuel-supply, equipment, permitting and community challenges of its own.

A third path to data center interconnection is taking shape. Across the country, utilities and grid operators are creating clearer ways for large loads that can reduce the risks they bring to the system. Depending on the market, a data center might guarantee a minimum payment, agree to reduce its grid demand when the system is strained, provide better operating data or contract for new generation and storage.

These policies differ, but they share one idea: If a data center wants faster or more certain access to power, it should give the grid greater certainty in return.

That is a better approach than advancing projects mainly because they promise jobs or investment. Projects that make their demand more predictable, controllable and financially secure should have a clearer path to service.

Utilities often need to invest in substations, transmission equipment and other infrastructure years before a data center reaches full operation. They are building against forecasts that can change, and their queues may include projects that never get built.

Another utility might choose a different percentage or structure. But a company asking the grid to reserve an extraordinary amount of power should be willing to stand behind that request financially. This protects existing customers from paying for infrastructure built around demand that does not materialize. It also helps serious projects distinguish themselves from speculative ones.

Other markets are focused on how data centers behave once they connect.

A large facility can draw hundreds of megawatts, change its consumption quickly or disconnect during a grid disturbance. At that scale, it’s part of how the power system operates, whether it considers itself a grid participant or not.

Texas is responding with clearer technical and operating expectations. A facility that meets the Electric Reliability Council of Texas’ ride-through requirements clears one important gate on the path to energization, alongside required modeling, stability and interconnection reviews. Through controllable-load and bring-your-own-generation pathways in ERCOT's new Batch Zero process, developers can elect dispatchable curtailment under ERCOT’s real-time control in exchange for earlier energization.

Meaning, a data center must be able to reduce what it takes from the grid when the system is strained. It could curtail consumption, switch to onsite resources or coordinate several assets. What matters is that the response is available when promised.

Southwest Power Pool is making a similar trade through its High Impact Large Load framework. Developers that accept real-time forecast reporting, ramp-rate limits and remote-disconnect capability can have their interconnection study completed within 90 days, clearing the way for an agreement soon afterward.

In both markets, developers give the grid better visibility and more control during constrained periods. In return, they receive a faster and more predictable process.

PJM Interconnection is approaching the challenge through resource adequacy that prioritizes whether the region has enough capacity to meet demand when the system is under the most stress.

Under PJM’s proposed Interim Resource Adequacy Service framework, certain new large loads could connect even when the region doesn’t have enough capacity to serve all forecast demand at its traditional reliability standard. Loads that didn't bring qualifying new capacity or receive an applicable capacity allocation could have been required to reduce their grid consumption during limited emergency conditions. FERC rejected most of the framework in October on jurisdictional grounds, but it encouraged states to decide which retail customers are curtailed when the system is stressed, which keeps the same trade on the table.

A data center could reduce that exposure by bringing qualifying generation, storage or demand-side resources. Otherwise, it would accept service that is less firm than what existing customers receive.

Bringing new capacity does not make transmission and distribution constraints disappear. A project may still need local upgrades, engineering studies and regulatory approvals. But if the system is short on capacity, a new load should either help fill the gap or be prepared to reduce its demand when the shortage becomes critical.

Different rules, one underlying question.

Each market is responding to its own constraints, but the question at the heart of these efforts is the same: What can a data center guarantee in exchange for access to power?

Federal regulators are now pushing that question across the country. In June, FERC directed all six regional grid operators under its jurisdiction to justify their existing large load tariffs or propose reforms. States are moving in parallel through large load tariffs and other requirements intended to ensure that data centers pay their share and manage their impact on the grid.

Today, developers learn the answer after spending time and money evaluating a site. Requirements can differ significantly from one utility or region to the next, making it difficult to compare locations, price the obligations attached to service and design the right energy infrastructure from the beginning.

Utilities and grid operators can make this process clearer. A developer should understand early on how specific commitments affect access to power.

The answer isn’t one national tariff or one required technology. Every grid has different needs, and data centers can provide flexibility through batteries, onsite generation, workload management or a combination of resources.

Utilities and grid operators should define a clear menu of performance-based pathways. Developers should know what they can commit, what the grid will require and what faster or more certain service they can receive in return.

Clearer options will help utilities separate real projects from speculative requests, allow developers to understand the cost of speed before committing to a site and give regulators a stronger basis for determining whether an arrangement protects reliability and existing customers.

The fastest data centers will not necessarily be the ones that find a way around the grid. They will be the ones that give the grid enough confidence to move forward with them.

KPop Demon Hunters on 4K Blu-ray costs less than a month of Netflix Premium

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The 4K Criterion Collection set is available to pick up at Barnes and Noble for $25.

The 4K Criterion Collection set is available to pick up at Barnes and Noble for $25.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Buying the 4K Blu-ray disc version of KPop Demon Hunters was already going to be a wise idea for some people, especially those who primarily keep their $26.99 Netflix Premium subscription just to watch the hit animated flick in 4K. But at just $24.99 from Barnes and Noble instead of its original $49.95 price, it’s easier than ever to recommend.

One important thing to note: it’s unavailable to ship from Barnes and Noble, but you can find a discounted copy at most retail stores, and the site makes it easy to reserve a copy online to pick up later. Amazon was matching the price earlier today, but is currently offering a slightly less tempting deal.

The Criterion Collection version of the film recently launched, and comes with a bunch of extras in a gorgeous set. In addition to the 4K digital master, complete with Dolby Vision HDR and Dolby Atmos sound, you also get two standard 1080p Blu-rays, one containing the film, and the other packed with special features (a full feature-length animatic, a making-of documentary, lyric videos, and more). It includes physical goodies, too, like collectible postcards, as well as an essay by Polygon alum Karen Han.

Kpop Demon Hunters was on my list of physical movies to buy, and this deal just turned it into a must-buy ahead of the holiday season.

powerhouse parramatta’s steel-latticed museum opens with art by es devlin and james turrell

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

behind its intricate white exoskeleton, the sydney museum welcomes its first visitors with an ambitious program of exhibitions.

Powerhouse Parramatta, the new museum designed by Moreau Kusunoki and Genton in Western Sydney, Australia, will officially open to the public on November 7th, 2026. Following years of construction along the Parramatta River, the monumental cultural building, with its lattice facade, is ready to welcome its first visitors, with five inaugural exhibitions including immersive installations by Es Devlin and James Turrell.

The opening marks a significant moment for Western Sydney, bringing the region its first state cultural institution and the largest museum ever built in New South Wales. Spanning more than 18,000 square meters of public and exhibition space, the building introduces a new home for the Powerhouse’s collections of art, design, science, and technology. More than 3,000 objects will be presented across the opening exhibitions, with one designed by OMA/AMO.

The museum‘s distinctive steel exoskeleton has already established a striking presence along the Parramatta River. Designed by French and Japanese architecture studio Moreau Kusunoki with Australian practice Genton, the building comprises two large volumes enclosed by a white structural lattice. Its outward-facing framework allows for expansive, column-free interiors, giving the museum flexibility to accommodate installations of varying scales.

Beyond the galleries, the project introduces new public spaces along the waterfront, including a landscaped square connecting the museum to the river. A rooftop garden, observatory, and spaces for educational programming extend its use beyond conventional exhibition halls. With the building now complete, attention turns to the artists and collections that will occupy its vast interiors.

Among the opening presentations is The Turning, the first major Australian exhibition by British artist Es Devlin. Known for her large-scale installations and stage designs, Devlin brings together five works exploring the relationships between human perception, collective memory, and the natural world.

The exhibition includes Forest of Us, a mirrored labyrinth that draws parallels between the branching structures of human lungs and trees, and Memory Palace, a three-dimensional landscape tracing moments that have shaped human understanding across 75,000 years. In Collective Portrait, visitors are invited to sit opposite strangers and draw one another, contributing to an evolving installation. Other works explore drawing and the biodiversity of the Sydney region, inviting audiences to take an active role in the exhibition.

American artist James Turrell also contributes to the opening program with Shangri La (Over The Hump), a monumental light installation presented as part of Task Eternal, an exhibition examining humanity’s fascination with flight and space exploration.

The work belongs to Turrell’s celebrated Ganzfeld series, which uses light and color to alter the viewer’s perception of space. Visitors enter an environment of shifting hues, where the horizon appears to disappear and familiar boundaries become difficult to distinguish. The installation draws on Turrell’s longstanding research into human vision and sensory experience, offering an encounter with the uncertainty of depth and distance.

Elsewhere, the opening program includes The Mall, developed with OMA/AMO to examine the cultural history of shopping centers, and Overflow, a First Nations-led exhibition exploring relationships with water across New South Wales. An immersive children’s exhibition, The Dark, completes the five inaugural presentations.

The museum’s public opening also begins a 23-day festival of performances, workshops, and conversations, bringing activity into the galleries and surrounding spaces. Together, the exhibitions offer the first opportunity to experience Powerhouse Parramatta as a functioning cultural institution, with its architecture now serving the artists, collections, and communities it was designed to accommodate.

name: Powerhouse Parramatta | @powerhousemuseum lead designer: Moreau Kusunoki | @moreau.kusunoki local architect: Genton engineering: Arup location: Parramatta, Sydney, Australia
