14 TV shows canceled or ending in 2026, from Palm Royale to Ridiculousness

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

14 of the 26 series on TV Insider's list of 2026 cancellations and endings, from Palm Royale to The Steve Wilkos Show.

Most viewers picture a cancellation as a sudden decision, made in the middle of a season, that takes a show off the air before anyone sees it coming. Many of this year's endings don't fit that picture. CBS announced in March 2025 that The Neighborhood would get one last season, according to Variety. The CW announced a final season for All American in June 2025, according to TheWrap. Both networks gave their shows a full season to close their stories, so anyone who counts them as 2026 cancellations is looking at decisions made in 2025. For these shows, 2026 is the year the goodbye airs, not the year the network decided. Their fans had months of notice.

Other cancellations turn on more than a show's own numbers. Networks weigh each series against the new shows they want to launch, and a network can't add a show without dropping another. "We aggregate all the numbers, and we have to make those tough decisions in order to make room for new shows," CBS Entertainment President Amy Reisenbach said, as reported by The Washington Times. Ratings still count in that calculation, but so do the network's plans for the next season. A show that ends is not always a show that failed, and a weak show is not always the only one at risk. The single word "canceled" covers endings with very different causes, and readers learn more by asking what pushed each show out than by counting the losses.

TV Insider set out to catalog the series that were canceled or are ending during 2026, from Ridiculousness and The Late Show with Stephen Colbert to The Way Home and Palm Royale. The catalog counts endings of every kind, so scheduled farewells appear alongside abrupt cuts. That mix is worth keeping in view, because a comedy in its debut season and a staple that has aired for decades end up under the same heading for very different reasons. The slides that follow take each ending on its own terms. They look at what it cost the network to keep the show, what the network wanted in its place, and whether the audience ever had a say.

Apple $AAPL TV ended Palm Royale early, even though the comedy had drawn plenty of Emmy attention. The streamer gave the series only two seasons, according to TV Insider. Its first season received 11 Emmy nominations, 9to5Mac reports, and its one win went to its theme music. The show was also nominated for Outstanding Comedy Series, but that nomination did not bring it a trophy. Palm Royale's single award honored its opening credits rather than its writing or its cast.

Online ratings put Palm Royale squarely in the middle, with a 60% average on Rotten Tomatoes and a score of 6.8 out of 10 on IMDb, according to 9to5Mac. Those scores show mild approval but little excitement, and they fall well short of the enthusiasm that surrounded Apple's comedy hits, such as Ted Lasso and The Studio. The second season ended in January, and Apple chose not to order a third. Fans who finished the latest episodes now know that those episodes were the last.

Viewers are left to weigh the nominations against the lukewarm ratings on their own. Together they describe a comedy that impressed Emmy voters but never won over audiences to the same degree.

Anne Rice's Talamasca: The Secret Order is a short, finished show that a viewer can watch from start to end in a weekend. AMC gave the series one season, according to TV Insider. That season ran six episodes and ended with a Nov. 23 finale, The Hollywood Reporter reports, and news of the cancellation came out on March 28, 2026. The show counts as a 2026 cancellation because word of its end arrived that year, not because it aired then. Viewers who finished the season in November learned months later that no more episodes were coming. Those six episodes are all there will be.

AMC is cutting a spinoff, not walking away from Anne Rice. A canceled spinoff can look like a network losing faith in a property, but AMC's main Anne Rice series, Interview With the Vampire, returns under its new title, The Vampire Lestat, for a third season June 7 on AMC and AMC+, according to The Hollywood Reporter. Belonging to the same Anne Rice franchise didn't save Talamasca. AMC is committed to the franchise, not to every show in it. The network does leave room for parts of the series to return, and it said in a statement that it expects "at least some of these characters, and the organization itself" to appear "in future expressions of the franchise." That promise covers pieces of Talamasca, not a second season. Fans who want more of the secret society will have to look to AMC's other Anne Rice shows.

CBS decided DMV's future while the comedy's first season was still on the air. The network ended the show after a single season, TV Insider reports, and the cancellation became public on March 27, 2026, according to TVLine. CBS made that call with only part of a first season to judge, and it chose not to wait for the rest before passing on a second. DMV had little time to settle into its time slot.

Viewers tend to read a fast cancellation as a sign that nobody was watching, but DMV had a large audience. The comedy averaged 5.7 million viewers, according to The Washington Times, which means millions of people were making time for a first-year show that CBS still dropped. An audience that size would count as a success on many schedules, yet it did not earn DMV a second season on CBS. On this network, a new comedy needed more than millions of weekly viewers to return. A shortage of viewers is hard to name as the reason for the decision.

The speed of the decision also shapes what fans get to see. TVLine reports that DMV's last episode will serve as the series finale. Fans will get one season and no more, and the episode that closes that season now has to stand as the end of the show. Viewers who are still watching DMV this year know where the series stops before they reach it.

The Late Show with Stephen Colbert was a late-night staple on CBS for 11 seasons before the network canceled it, according to TV Insider. Colbert was not leaving a fading show. When his final episode aired in May, no other late-night talk show on broadcast TV drew better ratings, according to the Chicago Sun-Times. CBS did not end the show for lack of an audience, and that makes the network's stated reason worth testing.

CBS called the cancellation "purely a financial decision," as reported by the Chicago Sun-Times. The word "purely" leaves no room for any other motive. A show can lead its time slot and still cost a network more than it earns, so strong ratings alone can't prove CBS wrong. The network's choice of replacement shows what its money case means. Comics Unleashed took over the 10:35 p.m. hour, and its host, Byron Allen, makes payments to CBS for the right to broadcast it. The network gave up a show it paid for and took on one that pays the network. On those terms the financial explanation holds together, but it doesn't show that money was the only consideration.

The doubts about CBS's reasoning come from the calendar. Colbert publicly described the $16 million that CBS paid Trump to settle a lawsuit as a bribe, and news of the cancellation followed just two days later, according to the Chicago Sun-Times. A gap that short invites people to read the cancellation as retaliation. Paramount $PARA Global's merger with Skydance Media was awaiting federal approval at the time. That gave the company a reason to avoid friction with the Trump administration, though it doesn't prove a political motive.

CBS's financial case and the timing can both be true, and neither the ratings nor the Comics Unleashed deal settles which one drove the decision.

CBS decided to end The Neighborhood while viewers were still watching it in large numbers. The network kept the comedy on its schedule for eight seasons, according to TV Insider, and viewers stayed with it through that long run. In its seventh season, the show was averaging 6.4 million viewers an episode by CBS's 35-day multiplatform count, according to Variety. With an audience that large, the reason for the ending has to lie somewhere other than the ratings.

CBS's own leadership praised the show's audience in public. "’The Neighborhood’ is a strong ratings performer for CBS and one of the top comedies on TV," Amy Reisenbach, president of CBS Entertainment, told Variety. Yet The Neighborhood was missing from the group of nine shows that CBS brought back in February 2025. A network that praises a show's ratings and then passes on it at renewal time is weighing something other than demand.

The reported reason is money. Variety reports that sources pointed to the cost of continuing the show as the main factor. Those sources are unnamed, and CBS's own announcement said nothing about cost. If the cost explanation holds, the show's popularity did not protect it. Its strong ratings earned The Neighborhood a last season, not a longer life.

CBS may not be done with the show's characters. A spinoff centered on the roles Marcel Spears and Sheaun McKinney play is in development at the network, according to Variety, but CBS has not given it a formal go-ahead. Fans should treat the project as a possibility rather than a promise. If it moves forward, viewers who followed the original show for eight seasons will have a reason to stay with CBS after the finale.

CBS gave Watson only two seasons, according to TV Insider. The Washington Times reports that CBS confirmed the end of the Morris Chestnut drama in late March 2026, several weeks after its second season returned on March 1. The network did not give the show a farewell season. It ended the series a few weeks into the second season's return, before fans had settled back into the new episodes.

Watson's audience over the full season was large by any ordinary measure. The drama averaged 6.4 million viewers in the 2025-26 season and ranked No. 74 among the season's 100 most-watched shows, according to Variety figures reported by The Washington Times. Most viewers would call a show with that many viewers a hit, and few would expect a network to cancel it after its second year.

CBS judges a show against the rest of its own schedule, and its standard is steep. Amy Reisenbach, CBS Entertainment President, told The Washington Times that "it's a high bar on CBS." A drama can draw a large audience by industry standards and still fall short on CBS's lineup. A place among the season's 100 most-watched shows did not clear the bar the network sets. CBS measured Watson against its own programs, not against television as a whole.

For Watson's fans, the show's healthy season numbers offered no protection on CBS.

All-American lasted through years of upheaval at the CW. The network gave the drama eight seasons, according to TV Insider. In 2022, shortly before Nexstar's deal for the network went through, the CW dropped 10 of its original series, TheWrap reports, and that was triple the number it cancels in a typical year. A cut that deep shows a network reshaping its lineup, and All-American stayed on the schedule for years afterward.

Viewers were not leaving All-American when the CW decided to end it. They were arriving. The drama finished its seventh season with a 71% larger audience of adults ages 18 to 49 than it had when the season began, according to TheWrap. Networks most want to reach those younger viewers. The CW answered that growth with a farewell season rather than a longer run, which suggests the network set the end point for some reason other than the size of the audience.

The CW also chose not to extend the franchise beyond the original series. It canceled the spinoff All American: Homecoming, TheWrap reports, so the main show ends without a sister series to carry its world forward. Nothing in the drama's ratings points to a show the CW had to drop. The ending looks more like a decision about the network's own direction than a verdict on All-American, though the CW's exact reasons remain its own. Fans get a final season to see the story through, which is more than a show in decline usually receives.

Strong reviews were not enough to keep Going Dutch on the air. Fox canceled the comedy after only two seasons, according to TV Insider. Of all the scripted shows on Fox, Going Dutch alone faced real doubt about a return, ScreenRant reports, and Fox settled that doubt with a cancellation. Fox had cast Denis Leary in the lead, but a familiar star could not draw the audience the show needed to last.

Critics and viewers reached very different conclusions about Going Dutch. Reviewers gave it an 85% critics score on Rotten Tomatoes, which means most of them recommended it, according to ScreenRant. Viewers judged it far more harshly, and their audience score of 54% counts as rotten on the site. Fox's decision matched the audience's negative verdict rather than the reviewers' praise. A network has to fill its time slots with viewers, and a show cannot stay on the air on good notices alone.

The cancellation shows how few people watched Going Dutch, and it says little about the quality of the show. Fans who enjoyed it have no reason to treat Fox's decision as a judgment against their taste. Anyone curious about the comedy that critics recommended can still watch it. Fox will not make a third season, but Hulu streams the second, according to ScreenRant.

The Hallmark Channel is ending The Way Home after four seasons, according to TV Insider, and that is a long run for a drama that began at another company. The Arizona Daily Star reports that the series was originally developed at Netflix $NFLX before it came to Hallmark. A project that started elsewhere became a Hallmark series that lasted for years, so its ending closes a show that found a home rather than one that never caught on.

The series reached Hallmark because one executive was willing to vouch for it. Lisa Hamilton Daly brought the show to the network, the Arizona Daily Star reports, and her support carried real risk for a channel with firm habits. Andie MacDowell, the show's star, said the series "was a reach for Hallmark. It sort of broke down some boundaries they had and really took a chance on something a little bit juicier." MacDowell has worked with Hallmark for seven seasons, so her judgment comes from someone who knows the network's limits from the inside.

The Way Home has never tried to settle every mystery it raises. Executive producer Alexandra Clarke has described a series that answers some of its questions and leaves others open, according to the Arizona Daily Star. Viewers heading into the last episodes may want to keep that in mind, because the show's makers have treated open questions as part of the story from the start.

MTV's cancellation of Ridiculousness ends production of the clip show while keeping it on the air. The network has built a deep archive of the series, airing 46 seasons, according to TV Insider. KTLA reports that the show premiered on Aug. 29, 2011, and MTV produced that entire run of seasons in the years since. That stockpile is why Ridiculousness carries a 2026 end date at all. MTV stopped production in 2025, and episodes it had already finished will air to close out the 2026 season. The network made its decision in 2025, and it will run out of new episodes to air in 2026. MTV is spending down a backlog of episodes made before production stopped, rather than presenting a final season written as a goodbye.

Ridiculousness will remain available after MTV airs its last first-run episode. Viewers can keep streaming select seasons on Paramount $PARA+, according to KTLA, so fans can keep watching on demand long after the show leaves MTV's lineup of new programming. For a viewer, this cancellation means no new episodes. It does not take away the episodes MTV already made, and a fan who missed years of the show still has a large catalog to work through.

MTV is winding Ridiculousness down rather than erasing it.

Law & Order: Organized Crime ended in stages rather than in one sudden cut. TV Insider counts it as a five-season NBC series, yet its final season never aired on NBC. That season streamed on Peacock instead, according to Variety. NBC moved the show off its broadcast schedule before it announced any cancellation. Viewers saw the last episodes through streaming rather than on broadcast TV.

After that last season, the show spent most of a year neither returning nor officially ending. Variety reports that the cancellation came out on April 16, 2026, about ten months after the final season ended. By then the news confirmed the fate of a show that was already off the air. It did not cut one that was still running. The move to Peacock turned out to be the show's last stop, and the April announcement was a formality for fans who had long since watched the final episode.

The cancellation ends the spinoff, while the older series where its lead character began keeps going. Christopher Meloni played Elliot Stabler, according to Variety, a character who first appeared on Law & Order: Special Victims Unit before getting a series of his own. Special Victims Unit is in its 27th season, so the series that introduced Stabler has outlasted the one built around him. Fans of the character still have that show on the air.

NBC's crime franchise goes on without this branch.

Access Hollywood is the kind of program viewers expect to find on their TV schedule indefinitely, a fixture of the daily lineup rather than a series anyone watches for signs of an ending. Viewers have had 30 seasons of the show to settle into that habit, according to TV Insider. In the summer of 2025, the show's future still looked secure. CNBC reports that as of July 2025, Access Hollywood was set to continue its broadcasts. Nothing public explains when that outlook changed or why.

The easy explanation is that NBCUniversal is clearing out its older entertainment-news shows and Access Hollywood was simply next, but the most recent cut points the other way. When the end of E! News was announced in July 2025, both Access Hollywood and its spinoff Access Daily were slated to keep broadcasting, according to CNBC. That decision spared the Access shows rather than starting their decline. The two brands are also headed to different corporate homes. E! is moving to Versant, the Comcast $CMCSA spinoff, while Access Hollywood is listed under NBCUniversal. With the two endings on opposite sides of Comcast's split, tying Access Hollywood to the fate of E! News would mean drawing a link that the record doesn't show.

Without that borrowed explanation, what remains is a three-decade run ending with no public reason, and no cause or final air date accompanies the news. For a show on the air this long, that silence leaves viewers with nothing to weigh.

The fair response is to hold off on a verdict rather than borrow one.

Karamo is ending as an established show, not as a new series that never found its footing. It aired for four seasons, according to TV Insider, well past the point where a network has to decide whether a new show deserves a second year. A series reaches a fourth season only if someone chooses to bring it back after each of the earlier ones. Keeping a show on the air that long takes a fresh decision to continue at the end of every season, not one early bet that is then forgotten.

The length of that run changes what the ending means for viewers. The people who watched Karamo are losing a show that had been part of their schedule for years. They aren't losing a pilot they might have skipped or a debut they barely had time to sample. For them, the cancellation breaks a routine, and that kind of loss is harder to shrug off than the end of a show that came and went within a few months.

Karamo is an NBCUniversal show, and its cancellation comes this far into its run. An ending at that stage concerns where the show could go next, not whether it ever worked. Four seasons had already answered that question.

Karamo goes off the air as a show that made it.

Some of this year's endings came before a show had a chance to settle in, but The Steve Wilkos Show is ending after a run that most series never reach. The talk show was on the air for 19 seasons, according to TV Insider. That is a long career for any program on television. This cancellation closes a show with years of episodes behind it, not one that disappeared after a brief tryout. Fans who watched it lose a program that had been on the schedule for a long time. They are not losing a debut they barely had time to sample, and the show's full run is already on the record.

The Steve Wilkos Show belongs to NBCUniversal's group of programs. That tells viewers which company the show sat under, but it does not tell them why the show is ending. The length of the run does not explain the timing either. A show can last for many seasons and still end for reasons that have little to do with its record. Cost, ratings and plans for the time slot all remain open questions, and none of them should be filled in by guesswork. Until a reason is made public, the one certain fact about the cancellation is how long the show lasted.

The cancellation does not shorten that run. The show lasted, and few TV shows can say the same.

A minor Nier: Automata update inexplicably increased the game's Steam install size from 50GB to a stonking 366GB, and the official Nier series account has now confirmed that something's gone horribly, hilariously wrong.

"We have identified an issue following the latest update for NieR:Automata on Steam, released on November 6, 2026 (JST), where installation requires significantly more storage space than intended," a recent Twitter post from the account reads.

"We are currently investigating the cause and working to resolve the issue. We apologize for the inconvenience. As a temporary workaround, you can download the base game using the intended amount of storage space by going to 'Properties' → 'DLC' in Steam and unchecking each DLC item."

The good news is that the game seems to be perfectly playable, assuming you can spare the space or sever those DLCs. An incredible display of video game spaghetti all around.

Sky-high hype: ‘Pilot Picasso’ draws massive GTA 6 mural in 8-hour flight over Arkansas

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Screenshot by Destructoid via @FlightRadar24

The anticipation for Grand Theft Auto 6 has gotten so high, you can even see it if you look up in the air. Kind of.

A 28-year-old commercial pilot and content creator took to the skies over Arkansas in the US for eight hours to create a mural dedicated to GTA 6, saying “I’ve been waiting 11 years for this moment.” It’s actually been 13 years since GTA 5 first released, but the idea still stands.

Benjamin McDaniel, known as The Pilot Picasso, often uses flight trackers to create art in the sky, as evidenced by his Instagram page that’s overflowing with creations. In the past, he’s done all sorts of artwork inspired by different passions such as Spider-Man, The Lord of The Rings, The Odyssey, and even other games like Skyrim.

This time, he wanted to highlight his excitement for Rockstar Games’ latest title, and the detail in the final image is actually pretty wild, caught by @FlightRadar24. Along with spelling out the full GTA 6 title, the air-painting also includes a helicopter, palm trees, an alligator, a sports car, and of course, a non-descript baddie in sunglasses holding a gun.

Apparently, this sort of thing is done by inputting a flight pattern and then following along with lines dictated by tech on-board the plane, but that doesn’t make it any less impressive, especially when you consider the dedication it takes to fly around in circles like this for eight hours or more.

We’re now less than two weeks away from GTA 6 launching, so I’d imagine that the marketing and other publicity stunts like this will be ramping up leading up to release on Nov. 19. I’m all for it.

Now, we can all only hope that this sort of thing can be recreated in GTA 6 itself with in-game planes.

Tech stocks rebound after a massive AI selloff spread to Asia

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

The Nasdaq rose 1.9% Friday morning after a 5.5% drop put it in correction territory, while the Dow was down 99 points

The Nasdaq $NDAQ Composite rose 1.9% in morning trading Friday as chip and data center stocks recovered from a selloff on Thursday that cut about $2.5 trillion from the index and spread to Asian markets.

Micron $MU Technology stock climbed nearly 7% Friday, recovering part of Thursday's 17% slide, and Nvidia $NVDA stock gained 6% after dropping 9%. Intel $INTC and CoreWeave stock were also climbing. The S&P 500 gained 1%, but the Dow Jones Industrial Average lagged, off by 99 points for a 0.2% decline.

The Nasdaq Composite fell 5.5% on Thursday and finished over 10% under its latest peak, which puts it in correction territory. The S&P 500 shed 4.4%, and the Dow gave up upward of 1,800 points, a 3.4% slide. Those were the worst one-day losses for the three averages since April 2025, according to CNBC.

Tech-focused benchmarks across Asia declined on Friday, echoing the rout on Wall Street a day earlier. SoftBank stock lost 10% of its value in Tokyo, while indexes in Europe declined by over 1%. In South Korea on Thursday, Samsung Electronics stock dropped 7.5% and SK Hynix stock lost 9.5%.

Investors sold on worries that data center construction could slow after Democrats won the House, the Senate and key governorships, according to CNBC. Top Democrats have pushed to rein in the expansion of data centers ever since the election.

Texas Gov.-elect Gina Hinojosa, whose state is a major data center hub, said in an interview Thursday evening, “We will have laws to protect Texans, and we need to stop what we are doing with data centers until we have those protections,” according to CNBC.

The Bureau of Labor Statistics reported that employers added 165,000 jobs in October, and the unemployment rate was 4.1%. Economists polled by Dow Jones had expected a gain of 59,000 jobs and an unemployment rate of 4.2%, according to CNBC.

Investors had sold AI-related chip and data center stocks after the midterm elections on Thursday. Qualcomm $QCOM stock also fell after the chipmaker's earnings beat estimates but its outlook disappointed.

Hinojosa defeated Republican Gov. Greg Abbott $ABT in Texas, according to CNBC. Wells Fargo $WFC estimates Texas holds 27% of planned U.S. data center gigawatts. Republicans hold the majority in the Texas Legislature, which limits how much Hinojosa can change policy.

Despite Friday's gains, all three major averages are headed for weekly losses. The Nasdaq is down 3% this week, the S&P 500 is down 2%, and the Dow is down 1%.

DoJ's 'Super Intelligence Task Force' up and running

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Organization responsible for countering anyone who wishes to scupper the US's ambition for AI supremacy

The US Department of Justice (DoJ) says its Super Intelligence Task Force (SINTF) is now up and running, after president Donald Trump ordered the move in a memo last month.

Headed up by director of national intelligence Jay Clayton, as well as associate attorney general Stanley Woodward and associate deputy attorney general Stephen Weiss, SINTF's responsibility is to protect the US's lead in artificial intelligence development.

Thursday's announcement, which was littered with the "super intelligence" Trumpism, said the task force will ensure the US's SI/AI leadership remains uncompromised by foreign AI "abuse," including by state and state-backed groups, criminal enterprises, individuals, or corporate entities.

Per Trump's October 13 memo [PDF], SITF will take "affirmative measures, as appropriate," to ensure the US stays ahead in AI development. It did not elaborate on what this nebulous phrase meant, but it can be reasonably assumed this means a breadth of proactive steps.

The memo also stated that the task force will organize the DoJ's criminal and civil lawsuits involving AI, recommend enforcement priorities concerning public safety and AI misuse, develop prosecution guidance for unlawful AI conduct, and feed into legislative proposals to prevent "burdensome regulation."

Addressing a Wall Street Journal tech conference this week, Pentagon CTO and SITF vice chair Emil Michael said the team would release details about its AI regulatory landscape later this month.

The task force will also shoulder various cross-departmental responsibilities, such as "deconflicting investigations, cases, and other matters" among departments and the offices of US attorneys.

According to quotes from senior officials, chief among the concerns facing ordinary US citizens are AI attacks that target individuals, as well as those that aim to disrupt critical infrastructure and other national security interests.

"The creation of the DOJ-SITF marks an important moment for the Department to help advance SI technologies and, at the same time, protect American innovation," said Weiss.

"The Department also has a responsibility to utilize law enforcement tools at our disposal to deter improper use of SI, harden regular reporting on SI risk and response, and ensure companies exercise vigorous internal controls so that SI technology behaves as intended. The Department is committed to protecting all Americans and our business interests."

Trump compelled the US to start using the term "super intelligence" in September, claiming "artificial intelligence," the widely used and original term coined in the mid-1950s, was a better descriptor for the technology's capabilities.

The US government's dedicated website for SI states that "America is in a race for Super Intelligence. We intend to win it."

It also highlights a number of initiatives the government has taken to promote its AI supremacy, including legal guidance to streamline federal approvals, leading to more data centers being built.

The pivot toward renaming the technology in all official capacities raised confusion at the time.

A somewhat contradictory move, the Trump administration heavily promotes its own AI innovators, but in one swoop essentially antiquated the trading name of arguably its most recognizable AI lab, OpenAI.

One financier previously floated the idea that Trump ordered the shift to boost the value of .si domain names, many of which have recently been registered using MAGA-related terms and resold for millions of dollars. There is no categorical evidence that the investments are linked to the Trump family, however. ®

Marianne Moore on the Three Elements of Persuasive Writing

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Every month, I spend hundreds of hours and thousands of dollars keeping The Marginalian going. For two decades, it has remained free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human and alive thanks to patronage from readers. I have no staff, no interns, not even an assistant — a thoroughly one-woman labor of love that is also my life and my livelihood. If it makes your own life more livable in any way, please consider aiding its sustenance with a one-time or loyal donation. Your support makes all the difference.

You can also become a spontaneous supporter with a one-time donation in any amount:

Partial to Bitcoin? You can beam some bit-love my way: 197usDS6AsL9wDKxtGM6xaWjmR5ejgqem7

Need to cancel a recurring donation? Go here.

For an act of resistance to the tyranny of algorithms, try the Marginalian newsletter—undistracted notes on the search for meaning, free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human since 2006. (Here is an example.)

Several years ago, rummaging through the archives of the Academy of American Poets, I came upon a box labeled “Ballots 1950” — the record of the secret vote by the chancellors the year the Academy’s prestigious fellowship was awarded to E.E. Cummings, catapulting him into renown. The voting process is a black box — no one outside the Academy ever finds out who else is in the running and by how much the winner wins.

Leafing through the ballots, one other name appeared over and over, so much so that I was impelled to count.

Marianne Moore had lost by one vote, never knowing how close she had come. It would be many more years until, at 77, she was finally awarded the fellowship.

Long before that, before she won the Pulitzer Prize and the National Book Award (sharing a table with Rachel Carson at the ceremony), Moore had set down her views on writing in a series of essays later collected in the out-of-print gem Predilections (public library). Pulsating through them is a reckoning with the impossible task of the writer — to weave tapestries of truth and meaning from the tenuous thread of words on the ramshackle loom of language.

In an essay titled “Feeling and Precision,” Moore writes:

Feeling at its deepest — as we all have reason to know — tends to be inarticulable. If it does manage to be articulate, it is likely to seem overcondensed, so that the author is resisted as being enigmatic or disobliging or arrogant.

How we name what we feel is not so much a matter of our writing style as of our style of being, because in order to articulate something we must first apprehend it and we apprehend every smallest thing with the whole person — with the frame of reference that is our entire life, the sum of our experience and memory. When “one of New York’s more painstaking magazines” asked Moore to distill her poetic style into a formula, she fought back the “dictatorial” reflex to quip:

You don’t devise a rhythm, the rhythm is the person, and the sentence but a radiograph of the personality.

And yet a personality can write with more or less persuasion — that is, write more or less well — depending on what the person brings to the writing. In another essay from the collection, Moore identifies the three psychological elements necessary for persuasive writing: “humility, concentration, and gusto.” A generation after Mark Twain assured his friend Helen Keller when she was accused of plagiarism that “substantially all ideas are second-hand, consciously and unconsciously drawn from a million outside sources,” she writes:

Humility… is armor, for it realizes that it is impossible to be original, in the sense of doing something that has never been thought of before. Originality is in any case a by-product of sincerity; that is to say, of feeling that is honest and accordingly rejects anything that might cloud the impression.

By “concentration” Moore means a kind of discipline — annealing the essence of the sentiment by cutting away all superfluous explanations, elaborations, and distractions of stylistic posturing, being maximally truthfully in the most minimal way possible. Observing that there is always a “helpless sincerity which precipitates a poem” and that a good poem is always “a concentrate,” she writes:

Concentration — indispensable to persuasion — may feel to itself crystal clear, yet be through its very compression the opposite… I myself would rather be told too little than too much.

Long before we had the language of extrinsic and intrinsic motivation, of finite and infinite games, Moore uses a lovely word, now dusty, for that peculiar private zeal propelling all creative work with its twin dynamos of discipline and deliverance: “gusto.” Echoing Rachel Carson’s abiding advice on writing — “If you write what you yourself sincerely think and feel and are interested in,” she had counseled a young writer, “the chances are very high that you will interest other people as well.” — Moore offers:

Gusto thrives on freedom, and freedom in art, as in life, is the result of a discipline imposed by ourselves. Moreover, any writer overwhelmingly honest about pleasing himself is almost sure to please others.

She maps the fundamental relationship between the three:

Humility is an indispensable teacher, enabling concentration to heighten gusto.

When creating in integrity with these three values, it ceases to matter how the work is received because the process of locating and articulating the truth as you feel it, the world as you see it, is its own reward. In what may be the best advice I have encountered on how to orient to your own work, Moore writes:

There are always objecters, but we must not be sensitive about not being liked or not being printed… The thing is to see the vision and not deny it; or care and admit that we do.

Complement with Walt Whitman on how to keep criticism from sinking your soul and Mary Oliver’s advice on writing, then savor the moving story of how Marianne Moore saved a rare tree with a poem.

Every month, I spend hundreds of hours and thousands of dollars keeping The Marginalian going. For two decades, it has remained free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human and alive thanks to patronage from readers. I have no staff, no interns, not even an assistant — a thoroughly one-woman labor of love that is also my life and my livelihood. If it makes your own life more livable in any way, please consider aiding its sustenance with a one-time or loyal donation. Your support makes all the difference.

7-year-old Pokemon fan furious that Magikarp got an SIR card before Gyarados

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A seven-year-old Pokemon fan has gone viral after demanding that The Pokemon Company give Gyarados the Special Illustration Rare card he believes the classic Water-type deserves.

Gyarados first appeared in the physical Pokemon Trading Card Game’s Base Set and has since received dozens of printings, including ex, GX, V, VMAX and Shining versions. However, collectors still have no physical Gyarados Special Illustration Rare, the full-art rarity marked by two gold stars in the modern era.

The complaint is especially focused on Magikarp’s Illustration Rare from Paldea Evolved. The Shinji Kanda card became one of the set’s most talked-about chase cards because its artwork hides a Gyarados in the background, making the contrast with its evolution’s lack of a SIR even more noticeable.

In the 53-second TikTok, the child asks, “Can somebody explain how Gyarados still doesn’t have a special edition Pokemon card?”

He points out that Gyarados has been part of the Pokemon Trading Card Game since the original Base Set and says the Pokemon has “loads of different cards over the years.” He then calls Gyarados “one of the coolest Pokemon ever” before turning his attention to Magikarp.

“Don’t get me wrong, I love that Magikarp card,” he says, arguing that it makes no sense for Magikarp to have such a striking Illustration Rare while Gyarados still lacks a Special Illustration Rare. He also pitches the kind of artwork he would like to see, imagining Gyarados bursting out of the ocean in a dramatic scene.

“Come on Pokemon, give Gyarados an SIR!” he concludes, before asking viewers which Pokemon should receive one next.

The clip was reposted by Guru Collects on X, where it was described as a child finally having “ENOUGH of the Gyarados disrespect.” The repost helped push the complaint beyond the original TikTok, with collectors joining the conversation over which overlooked Pokemon deserve premium artwork.

Pokemon TCG Pocket does include a Gyarados Illustration Rare, but that digital card is separate from the physical Pokemon TCG releases being discussed in the video. The Pokemon Company has not announced a physical Gyarados SIR, so the seven-year-old’s request remains a fan campaign for now.

I’m Intrigued By Carson Daly’s Today News (But It Has Me Worried About Those The Voice Rumors Again)

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Riley Green Just Picked The Only Singer He Could To Continue On The Voice After THAT Performance

Michael Che Is Always Asked About The Best And Worst SNL Hosts, And I Appreciate His Thoughtful Take

People Are Worried Kimmel Will Get Canceled. Meanwhile, The Tonight Show’s Out Here Doubling Down

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

You can’t deny it, Carson Daly is a very busy person who is prominently featured on the 2026 TV schedule. Between his work on Today and The Voice, he’s a constant on NBC. However, following his big update about his job on the news program, it’s got me all worried about those rumors regarding his work on the singing competition again.

On November 7, we’ll go from seeing Carson Daly five days a week on Today to six. While he's been working on Saturdays since September, he’ll officially be joining the Saturday Today team as the co-anchor alongside Laura Jarrett this weekend. He’s been working with the Today show for the last 13 years, and Libby Leist, the Executive Vice President of Today and Lifestyle, told NBC they’re “thrilled” viewers will “get to spend one more day of the week” with Daly. The co-anchor also shared his thoughts on the news, explaining:

I’m thrilled to add Saturday TODAY to my weekday morning schedule. My kids asked if I’ll still make pancakes. I told them yes, but I’ll be making them for Laura, Joe and Angie instead. I’m equal parts grateful and excited for this opportunity.

The announcement also clarified that along with this new role (which fans really wanted Daly to be in), the co-anchor will continue to host The Voice, which he’s an executive producer on as well. He’s also an EP on The Voice: Celebrity (but he is not the host). So, clearly, he’s going to make all this work, well, work. However, this new update that will take him up to working six days on the New York-based morning news show does have me thinking about all those The Voice rumors again.

There have been rumors about Daly potentially leaving The Voice for a while now. The host and his family live in New York, which is also where Today is filmed, while the singing competition is based in LA. That’s a lot, no matter how you look at it, and it’s not lost on me that two years ago, the co-anchor did say he couldn’t wait to “get off television” while also noting that his “time with [his] kids is the most precious.”

So, with this workload at Today officially going from five to six days a week, it would make sense if he were looking for a way to cut back in other avenues of his career. Plus, rumors about Daly wanting to do that have been circulating for quite some time.

Again, absolutely nothing is confirmed when it comes to the rumors about him potentially leaving The Voice. And the announcement about his gig on Saturday Today even clarified that he will keep working on the competition show.

Therefore, Carson Daly will still be on our screens a whole lot as he takes on this new gig at Today and keeps hosting The Voice. However, as he starts working Saturdays permanently, I’ll be curious to see if there ever was any merit to those rumors regarding his tenure on the singing competition.

Midterm elections will put AI and data centers on trial, but Big Tech may end up winning anyway

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

THIS WEEK IN ENTERPRISE by Robert Hof

It may be a little soon to determine the ultimate winners and losers among enterprise suppliers after the midterm elections, but the early line is: Big Tech wins and data center suppliers operators such as chipmakers and neoclouds lose. Or will everybody lose?

The day after the elections, stocks of many tech companies shot up, a bit paradoxically because investors apparently believe potential limits on AI and data centers could tamp down spending on compute and thus improve tech company margins that have made investors nervous. But then on Thursday, tech stocks almost across the board plunged back down even more for a collective loss of $2.5 trillion in market cap — the Nasdaq fell more than 5.5% — on more generalized worries that, well, the AI boom could slow. Suffice to say, investors are still sorting things out.

Marvell Semiconductor this week made a surprisingly huge acquisition with Lumentum, a bet on the AI boom continuing. That is, if it comes off: The drop in Lumentum’s stock price has some speculating it could hunt around for another offer.

And the AI boom indeed continues apace, judging from strong tech earnings, though even pre-election, they reflected investor wariness. AMD, Supermicro and Arista all beat easily, as well as Palantir and Kyndryl, whose stock rocketed 30%. But chipmakers Qualcomm, Arm and Globalfoundries didn’t get the love despite good quarters — especially in Thursday’s bloodbath.

All of which raises the question that Dave Vellante posed in his Breaking Analysis last weekend: Can enterprise AI pay for the boom? Dave and analyst Crawford Del Prete discussed the prospects.

Next week’s earnings reports include Cisco, CoreWeave, Applied Materials, Cerebras and Nebius, any or all of which could move markets.

Here’s all of this week’s enterprise and emerging tech news, analysis and commentary from SiliconANGLE and beyond:

Amazon, Microsoft and other tech giants close higher after Democrats’ midterm sweep No, data centers won’t get shut down, and AI will continue apace — just regulated as it needs to be, and we can hope it’s in the right way to protect people and communities and innovation all at the same time. We can walk and chew gum at the same time if we put our mind to it. Hard to imagine the Dems could do worse than Trump and the GOP have been doing for years now.

OpenAI makes new data center commitments, AI policy recommendations That was quick; we’ll see if it makes a difference.

Arsenal star Ezri Konsa was taken off in the win against Slavia Prague

Arsenal manager Mikel Arteta has spoken about Ezri Konsa's availability this weekend.

The defender was taken off in Arsenal's 2-1 win away at Slavia Prague in the Champions League this week, as the Gunners made hard work of the second half, after cruising into a two-goal lead.

With William Saliba now available once more, Arteta has confirmed Konsa's status ahead of action this Saturday.

When asked about Konsa's injury, Arteta replied that the England international is, in fact, “fine”.

“He was feeling much better today,” the Basque boss said, adding, “We took him out as a precaution but yesterday and today he is feeling good.”

Arsenal were pressed into signing the defender in the summer, after Saliba suffered a back injury at the World Cup, in the semi-finals with his native France.

Cristhian Mosquera filled in – starting for the first three matches of the season, including a Community Shield win in Cardiff over FA Cup winners, Manchester City – before Konsa made his debut against former employers Aston Villa off the bench in the second match of the Premier League campaign.

The 29-year-old has started virtually every match since, with Mosquera picking up an injury at Villa Park that evening.

Konsa, too, gave Arsenal an injury scare during the last international break, with England boss Thomas Tuchel having to manage his fitness across the Three Lions' Nations League fixtures.

The work-load on both centre-backs will surely be lessened now, however, given that Saliba is back and available for Arteta.

The Frenchman was an unused substitute during the Champions League win in Prague, with his minutes likely to be managed accordingly in the coming weeks.

Konsa is worth €50 million, according to Transfermarkt. Arsenal host Hull City this weekend when Premier League action resumes.

New 80-Minute Dune Movie Releases On Digital In Just 1 Month

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Dune franchise is expanding sooner than expected.

Frank Herbert's iconic 1965 sci-fi novel, which follows noble heir Paul Atreides going on a grand adventure on the desert planet Arrakis when his family is betrayed, has launched a sprawling series both on the page and on the screen. Herbert himself wrote five sequels while his son Brian wrote 15 spinoff novels with Kevin J. Anderson. In addition to comic book, game, and television adaptations, the series has been adapted into a 1984 David Lynch movie starring Kyle MacLachlan and a 2021 Denis Villeneuve trilogy starring Timothée Chalamet, which is set to continue with the upcoming Dune: Part Three.

Now, a listing on Apple TV has confirmed that the documentary Dune - The Real Story is set to debut on digital on December 7. That sees the movie dropping for at home viewers exactly two weeks before Dune: Part Three debuts in theaters on December 18. The 80-minute documentary follows the trials and tribulation involved in bringing Dune to the big screen.

The documentary primarily focuses on the franchise's history of film adaptations ahead of the Villeneuve trilogy (which also spawned the HBO spinoff series Dune: Prophecy). This includes the David Lynch movie (which was famously a critical and commercial flop, though it later grew to become a cult film), but it also focuses on attempts to mount Dune movies that ultimately fell through.

The most famous of these is the version that was set to be helmed by The Holy Mountain director Alejandro Jodorowsky. His expansive 10-hour vision included a star-studded cast that featured Orson Welles, Gloria Swanson, Salvador Dalí, David Carradine, Udo Kier, and Mick Jagger, music by Pink Floyd, and designs by H. R. Giger. While the movie never received funding, its pre-production process directly inspired many other iconic movies such as Alien, Raiders of the Lost Ark, Star Wars, and The Terminator.

The story of the Jodorowsky version has been told in detail in the 2013 documentary Jodorowsky's Dune.

Director Ridley Scott also attempted to make a Dune movie in between his sci-fi masterpieces Alien (which debuted in 1979) and Blade Runner (which debuted in 1982), which will be covered in Dune - The Real Story. The all-encompassing documentary was written and directed by Robin Bextor, a documentarian who has helmed a number of similar movies, including Peaky Blinders: The Real Story, Nosferatu: The Real Story, Wicked: The Real Story, Oppenheimer: The Real Story, Twisters: The Real Story, and The Exorcist: Untold. He is also known for music documentaries about subjects such as The Beatles, UB40, and Kiki Dee.

Identify the cover art while scratching off as little foil as possible.

Sign in to claim your place on the leaderboard!

Sign in to claim your place on the leaderboard!

The Real Story is just one indication that the Dune phenomenon is far from over. While the impending Part Three is meant to be the final installment of the Villeneuve trilogy, the franchise is already set to continue with Prophecy season 2, and it has the potential to be a box office success to the degree that more spinoffs are mounted. Warner Bros' confidence in its prospects is highlighted by the fact that they are running it in theaters on the same weekend as Marvel's Avengers: Doomsday, which has otherwise scared away all other wide release competitors.

Milan, Amorim è stato chiaro tra le righe: a gennaio servono rinforzi

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Il copione che il Milan ha messo in scena ieri a San Siro contro il Ferencvaros, è stato già visto diverse volte in questa stagione. Il minimo comun denominatore tra le peggiori partite dell'anno, ovvero le quattro gare perse fino a questo momento, è che si giocavano tutte a metà settimana. Ieri i rossoneri hanno perso 0-2 contro i Ferencvaros in Europa League, la terza sconfita su quattro gare nella competizione europea dopo Benfica e Bournemouth. A queste si aggiunge il crollo del secondo tempo nel turno infrasettimanale di una decina di giorni fa contro il Bologna. Quattro indizi fanno una prova, la rosa non è ancora pronta per sostenere a livello tecnico e anche mentale la doppia competizione. E lo ha confermato con grande lucidità e onesta anche Ruben Amorim ieri.

Al termine della gara di ieri a San Siro, dopo il clamoroso 0-2 contro il Ferencvaros che ha messo a nudo ancora una volta la difficoltà delle seconde linee del Milan oltre che dei problemi di approccio evidenti nelle gare che si giocano ogni tre giorni, il tecnico Ruben Amorim non si è trattenuto davanti ai microfoni di Sky Sport 24, esprimendosi con la consueta onestà e lucidità che lo contraddistingue. "Non siamo pronti per essere competitivi su due fronti. Dobbiamo lavorare sul nostro modo di vedere il calcio e su come approcciamo le partite. Non ci sono scuse stasera, non c’è niente che io possa dire. Di solito ho tanto da dire per difendere la nostra squadra, questa sera no. Dobbiamo essere più umili e capire cos’è successo". Ed è quella prima frase, quel "non siamo pronti per essere competitivi su due fronti" che ha scoperchiato il vaso di Pandora. Il Milan sta crescendo sotto diversi punti di vista e in campionato, nei fine settimana, si vede. Quando c'è bisogno di fare rotazioni, però, tutto crolla e anche i giocatori che sono considerati titolari sembrano risentirne: vedi Rabiot ieri, oggettivamente uno dei peggiori in campo.

Non ci vuole essere malizia, nè tantomeno si vuole fare polemica, semplicemente le parole di Ruben Amorim tra le righe portano un messaggio fondamentale che sembra evidente già da diverso tempo: nella sessione di mercato di gennaio il Milan ha bisogno di rinforzi. Probabilmente ogni reparto ha la necessità di trovare energie nuove e un elevamento delle qualità tecniche. Servirà rinforzare la difesa, perché De Winter e Pavlovic hanno praticamente sempre giocato e perché ogni tanto i limiti globali del reparto saltano fuori: in questo senso il nome forte rimane quello di Inacio, già cercato in estate. Serve trovare qualcuno sulle corsie esterne, specialmente a sinistra. Serve poi dare un ricambio a Gonçalo Ramos e permettere a Francesco Camarda di andare a giocare con continuità in un contesto che possa esaltare le sue qualità e con pressioni meno asfissianti. Anche qui ci sarebbe un nome gradito a Gerry Cardinale, quello del tifoso milanista Niccolò Tresoldi del Club Brugge. Ora, mancano ancora due mesi, ma sicuramente il club deve iniziare a muoversi in vista del prossimo mercato, con l'arma Krosche in più che potrà dare il suo vitale contributo.

Thank Goodness A Nobody Wants This Star And I Have The Same Weird Emotions About Morgan And Sasha

I finally had a chance to watch The Invite with my HBO Max subscription, and as someone who went through a divorce a few years ago, it felt like Déjà Vu. So many similar arguments, so many conversations. I didn't have to know about Olivia Wilde's divorce from Jason Sudeikis to know it was all rooted in experience.

The only part that didn't feel right for me was the ending. While there's insistence that the ending is ambiguous, I'm not too sure I agree, having lived a similar version of those events. Here's what I think, as well as what Wilde has said about the movie's ending.

The decision to move forward with a divorce made sense, as did sleeping in separate rooms. Then it ended with a tender piano duet and a show of intimacy that, frankly, wasn't there between my ex and me when we finally decided to end our marriage.

Obviously, there are tons of divorces every year, and many different scenarios. Maybe there were moments when people about to live separate lives indefinitely played the piano together and shared a tender cuddle before consulting a mediator or attorney, but that wasn't my experience.

To me, it only makes sense that Angela and Joe ended up working on their marriage and didn't split after all. I think for all the chaos of the trailer, seeing the ending with Joe finally playing his piano again shows that he's willing to change and move forward. It's the birth of a new stage of their relationship, and Angela joining him at the piano shows she's ready to embrace it as well.

Olivia Wilde told USA Today that the ending is left up to the audience, but admitted she and Seth Rogen had different ideas about what happened. Rogen thought that Joe and Angela would be together forever, while Wilde believed that two people who break up can still love each other.

‘I Just Kept Saying Thank You.’ Seth Rogen On Filming Intimate Scenes With Penélope Cruz

Seth Rogen's optimistic take could come from the fact that he mainly does comedy movies, but I'd like to think he has the same takeaway I do about the final moment. Sure, people who divorce can still "have love" for each other without being in love, but I think that comes over time. Not in the instantaneous decision to divorce, but that's just my thought.

Conversely, I think it would take a lot more for a couple who's been together a decade plus to end their marriage. To make such a big decision in one night because the sex therapist who lives upstairs said your relationship is broken? To me, that feels like the most unrealistic part of The Invite, though again, it's just my take.

The Invite is available to stream on HBO Max and is a great watch from earlier in the 2026 movie schedule for those who missed it. It's considerably less funny than I was led to believe, but I am ultimately glad that I watched it.

Star Trek is his main jam, but he also regularly reports on happenings in the world of Star Trek, WWE, Doctor Who, 90 Day Fiancé, Quantum Leap, and Big Brother. He graduated from the University of Southern Indiana with a degree in Journalism and a minor in Radio and Television. He's great at hosting panels and appearing on podcasts if given the chance as well.

FAA Begins Retiring Decades Old Floppy Disks from Air Traffic Control

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

An official website of the United States government Here's how you know

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPS A lock ( LockA locked padlock ) or https:// means you’ve safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

Federal Aviation Administration Press Office 800 Independence Avenue, SW Washington, DC 20591 United States

800 Independence Avenue, SW Washington, DC 20591 United States

WASHINGTON, D.C. - U.S. Transportation Secretary Sean P. Duffy today announced the Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) is continuing its historic modernization of America’s air traffic control system by replacing outdated floppy disks that contain vital weather and other critical information with a streamlined, 21st-century system.

The Enterprise – Information Display System (E-IDS) replaces four systems dating back to the 1980s that are in more than 400 FAA facilities. E-IDS provides a single, secure system that gives air traffic controllers faster access to information they need. It also will reduce costly repairs and workarounds while enhancing safety for the American flying public.

The first five facilities to receive these upgrades include:

The FAA plans to install E-IDS in 435 sites by the end of 2028.

“Good news America: floppy disks are out, 21st century tech is in,” said U.S. Transportation Secretary Sean P. Duffy. “The greatest nation in the world deserves a state-of-the-art air traffic control system, and under President Trump, we’re delivering just that. The installation of E-IDS is just the latest signal that America’s brand new air traffic control system is coming at lightning speed.”

Click HERE or above to watch a video on the new technology.

“E-IDS gives air traffic controllers the information they need to make timely decisions and keep the flying public safe,” said FAA Administrator Bryan Bedford. “We’re moving away from outdated, manual processes and replacing technology that once relied on floppy disks with a modern system that allows controllers to instantly share critical flight alerts, restricted airspace schedules and pilot reports across the National Airspace System.”

You can track the progress of the Brand New Air Traffic Control System (BNATCS) by state and airport on our Modern Skies website.

E-IDS is a digital display board that puts important operational information in one place so controllers can quickly see updates, status information, and alerts they need to keep air traffic moving safely and efficiently. This information includes notices about equipment issues and disruptions, air traffic conditions, weather impacts, and important advisories.

E-IDS will also improve how Pilot Reports (PIREPS) and Notices to Airmen (NOTAMs) are collected and distributed. PIREPs are first-hand reports on weather conditions from pilots to air traffic controllers. Both provide vital information on real-time pilot conditions and improve situational awareness for controllers.

Under Secretary Duffy’s leadership, the FAA is rapidly moving to make upgrades to our air traffic control system nationwide. We have already:

Bill Skarsgard Didn’t Need Much Convincing To Join Hideo Kojima’s Physint

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Hideo Kojima's Physint has a leading man in Bill Skarsgård, and the Hollywood actor recently spoke more about his part in the game. Billed as a spiritual successor to Kojima's Metal Gear Solid franchise, Physint is an action-espionage game that features Skarsgård alongside several other actors, like Charlee Fraser, Don Lee, and Minami Hamabe. Skarsgård's love for the Metal Gear Solid franchise and a chance to work with Kojima proved to be the deciding factors in taking on the role of the unnamed Physint protagonist.

"I played every single one," Skarsgård said to Josh Horowitz on the Happy Sad Confused podcast. "Starting from the one on PlayStation One all the way to the last one. I was very enamored by him [Kojima], the mythology and the universes that he creates, but also the mystique and the allure about him, as well. He is an auteur, but he's a video game auteur. He's fluent in filmmaking, and film, and cinema."

Skarsgård believes that Kojima's cinematic video games are a new medium that he's pioneering, and with Physint, the developer is getting closer to "something that's photorealistic and also interactive," the actor said. "I think he's a pioneer, and he keeps going out to untreaded waters."

Kojima is also a fan of Skarsgård, and he recently spoke about how he was introduced to him through a mutual acquaintance, Death Stranding 2: On the Beach composer Yoann "Woodkid" Lemoine. After traveling to Sweden to meet Skarsgard and his family, Kojima pitched him the lead role in Physint, which Skarsgard accepted.

While it looks like production on Physint is starting to pick up steam, it's still early days for the project. Kojima previously revealed that the script for it is ready, but there's still work to be done on motion-capture and filming performances. Sony was originally funding development on it, but after the PlayStation company pulled support for it, Kojima found a new home at Xbox for Physint. The developer has described Physint as the "biggest-selling" game that he could possibly create, and Xbox is also funding development on his upcoming horror game, OD Knock.

No, your progress and items are not transferable to any other game.

No, Ratchet & Clank: Ranger Rumble‘s ceasing development has no impact on other Ratchet & Clank games or PlayStation services.

Quota o RNG? Due modi opposti di misurare il caso

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sabato sera, a San Siro, al derby sono bastati pochi minuti per prendere una direzione precisa. Pulisic al 4', Rabiot al 10', poi Bastoni ad accorciare e Diego Moreira a chiudere il primo tempo sul 3-1. Chi in quei minuti teneva d'occhio un'app di risultati ha visto i numeri delle quote muoversi quasi in tempo reale: ogni gol riscriveva le probabilità della serata nel giro di pochi secondi. Esiste però un altro tipo di numero legato al caso che non si sarebbe mosso di un millimetro, qualunque cosa fosse successa in campo. È quello dei giochi da casinò online, dove l’esito non dipende da ciò che accade nel mondo reale ma da un algoritmo certificato. Lì non ci sono formazioni da studiare né statistiche da aggiornare: le probabilità sono fissate in partenza e restano identiche dal primo all’ultimo giro. Su una slot machine, nessun gol, nessun infortunio e nessun cambio tattico può spostare di un decimale ciò che accadrà al giro successivo. Sono due modi opposti di trattare l’incertezza, e capire la differenza aiuta a leggere meglio entrambi.

Una quota decimale è, prima di tutto, una probabilità travestita. Il calcolo è semplice: dividendo 1 per la quota si ottiene la probabilità implicita che il bookmaker attribuisce a un evento. Una quota di 2,00 corrisponde al 50%, una di 4,00 al 25%. Se si sommano le probabilità implicite di vittoria, pareggio e sconfitta, però, il totale supera sempre il 100%: quella differenza è il margine dell'operatore, il prezzo del servizio incorporato nei numeri. Dietro quella cifra lavora un modello statistico alimentato da una quantità enorme di dati: risultati storici, rendimento in casa e in trasferta, assenze, calendario, condizioni della squadra. Negli ultimi anni a questi ingredienti si sono aggiunti gli expected goals, la metrica che assegna a ogni tiro una probabilità di diventare gol in base a distanza, angolo, parte del corpo usata e tipo di azione. Un rigore, per esempio, vale tra 0,76 e 0,79 xG: anche il tiro più «facile» del calcio, statisticamente, non è mai una certezza.

Perché la partita stessa è un flusso continuo di informazioni nuove. Prima del calcio d'inizio il modello ragiona su medie e tendenze; dal primo minuto in poi deve incorporare ciò che sta accadendo davvero. Un gol nei primi cinque minuti cambia radicalmente lo scenario, perché lascia a una squadra quasi tutta la gara per difendere un vantaggio e costringe l'altra a scoprirsi. Basta rileggere il tabellino del derby per capire quanto lavoro abbiano dovuto fare gli algoritmi sabato sera: quattro gol in 43 minuti significano quattro ricalcoli profondi delle probabilità, a cui si sommano cartellini, sostituzioni e ogni singola occasione. In questo senso una quota assomiglia più al prezzo di un titolo in Borsa che a un numero fisso: è una stima che si aggiorna man mano che arrivano notizie, e che può essere rivista anche più volte in pochi minuti.

Su una slot online non c'è nessun evento esterno da interpretare. L'esito di ogni giro è stabilito da un generatore di numeri casuali, l'RNG, un algoritmo che produce sequenze imprevedibili e che associa a ciascuna una combinazione di simboli. Ogni giro è indipendente dal precedente: il sistema non ha memoria, non conosce la «forma» e non tiene conto di quanto tempo sia passato dall'ultima combinazione vincente. Sul mercato regolamentato italiano questo meccanismo non è lasciato alla fiducia. Prima di arrivare nel catalogo di un concessionario come AdmiralBet, ogni titolo viene certificato da laboratori indipendenti che verificano la casualità del generatore e la percentuale teorica di ritorno al giocatore, il cosiddetto RTP. Per i giochi di sorte a quota fissa la normativa fissa una soglia minima del 90%, e il valore deve essere dichiarato in modo trasparente. È importante capire che cosa rappresenta quel numero. Un RTP del 96% non descrive una singola sessione, ma il comportamento statistico del gioco su milioni di giri simulati: è una proprietà del software, non una previsione su ciò che accadrà a chi gioca in una sera qualsiasi.

La sintesi più efficace è questa: la quota è una stima, l'RTP è una specifica. Nel calcio nessuno conosce in anticipo la probabilità «vera» che il Milan vinca un derby. I modelli la approssimano con i dati disponibili e possono sbagliare, perché in campo entrano variabili che nessun archivio storico può prevedere fino in fondo, dalla giornata di grazia di un singolo giocatore a un episodio arbitrale. In una slot, al contrario, la distribuzione delle probabilità non viene stimata ma progettata. Il game designer decide a tavolino con quale frequenza compariranno determinate combinazioni, i laboratori di certificazione verificano che il software si comporti esattamente così, e quel comportamento resta identico per tutta la vita del titolo. Il caso esiste in entrambi i mondi, ma in uno viene interpretato, nell'altro viene costruito.

L'errore di ragionamento più diffuso nasce proprio dal trasferire la logica del calcio ai giochi basati su RNG. Nel pallone la forma conta davvero: una squadra che vince da cinque partite ha buone ragioni statistiche per essere considerata favorita alla sesta. Su una slot, invece, una lunga serie di giri senza combinazioni vincenti non rende «più probabile» la prossima. Ogni giro riparte da zero, con le stesse identiche probabilità del primo. È lo stesso principio della moneta lanciata in aria: dopo dieci teste consecutive, la probabilità che esca croce al lancio successivo resta il 50%. I matematici lo sanno da secoli, eppure è una delle intuizioni più difficili da accettare, perché la nostra esperienza quotidiana, sport compreso, ci abitua a cercare tendenze anche dove non ce ne sono.

Forse proprio questo: che l'incertezza del calcio è irriducibile, e che è anche il motivo per cui lo guardiamo. Nessun modello, per quanto sofisticato, avrebbe potuto scrivere in anticipo il primo tempo di sabato sera, con tre gol rossoneri in meno di tre quarti d'ora. Le quote possono solo inseguire la partita, mai precederla. Il caso di una slot, invece, è un caso ingegnerizzato, misurato e certificato prima ancora che qualcuno prema il pulsante. Due numeri che a prima vista sembrano parenti, una quota su uno schermo e una percentuale in una scheda di gioco, raccontano in realtà due filosofie opposte: una prova a prevedere il mondo, l'altra lo simula. Ed è proprio nel distinguerle che si trova la chiave per leggerle entrambe con lucidità.

The AI boom’s missing balance-sheet question: What has actually been verified?

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

GUEST COLUMN by Neil P. Osnato

I spent 13 years on the floor of the New York Stock Exchange. One lesson stayed with me long after the trading jackets disappeared: A transaction could be executed, documented and perfectly valid while the assumptions that made it attractive proved less durable than anyone expected.

That distinction matters in the artificial intelligence infrastructure boom. We can believe AI will transform productivity and still ask whether each financing, data-center expansion or long-term capacity agreement is supported by evidence that can survive the investment’s life.

The industry is making commitments across different clocks. Compute contracts may run for years. Debt can mature decades from now.

Servers have much shorter technology cycles. Power infrastructure, transmission upgrades and buildings may outlast several generations of the equipment they were built to support. Meanwhile, enterprise customers are still learning what they will use, how intensively they will use it and what they will pay.

None of this proves the investments are unsound. It does create a question that belongs beside revenue forecasts and capital budgets: What exactly has been verified at the moment an institution decides to rely on a promise?

An agreement to purchase compute can be commercially meaningful. It may support financing and demonstrate customer commitment. But it does not by itself establish that the underlying workloads will remain economically productive at the contracted scale, or that the customer will renew on similar terms once technology and prices change.

A serious review must separate the existence of the contract from the economic assumptions behind its duration, usage profile, counterparty capacity and renewal expectations. Contract enforceability and economic durability are related; they are not interchangeable.

A data-center campus can be announced, financed and partially constructed before all of its necessary electricity, cooling, network and equipment dependencies have become operational. A power purchase agreement is not itself proof of deliverability at the exact commissioning date. GPU orders are not proof that a facility can run the ordered cluster at the planned duty cycle.

For infrastructure planning, I use a simple sequence: requested load, evidenced load, deliverable load, energized load, utilized load and sustained demand. These are not six fancy names for the same megawatt. They describe different claims, each requiring different supporting records.

That distinction is already relevant to public grid decisions. Persistence Analytics Group has submitted comments in MISO’s Large Load Working Group proceedings on load forecasts, application and study processes, paired-resource studies and cost transparency. The recurring question is how planners preserve alignment when a proposed project’s capacity, timing or other material conditions change after an earlier study or commitment.

Financial engineering can provide meaningful protection. Minimum payments, parent guarantees, collateral requirements, termination provisions and residual-value commitments can shift or mitigate losses. They should be evaluated on their actual terms and credit strength.

But a contractual remedy is not the same as the continued usefulness of the underlying asset. A guarantee may support recovery from a counterparty while leaving the investor dependent on that counterparty’s resources, enforceability, timing and the value remaining after a disruption. Those are separate assumptions that deserve separate evidence.

This is especially important when apparently distinct exposures rely on a small set of interconnected customers, infrastructure providers, funding sources or technology assumptions. Diversifying contracts does not necessarily diversify the dependency underneath them.

Initial diligence usually asks whether a project is supportable at approval. A more durable approach asks what must remain true afterward — and which changes require the original case to be revisited before additional money is committed.

Consider a campus whose customer delays its ramp, whose power availability moves to a later year or whose equipment changes materially. Each event may be manageable on its own. But if the original financing or infrastructure decision assumed the earlier configuration, someone must decide whether the updated evidence still supports the next commitment.

That is a decision gate, not a prediction about whether AI succeeds. It calls for a documented chain: the representation being relied upon; the assumption embedded in it; the evidence supporting that assumption; the dependencies that must hold; the party bearing downside exposure; and the material change that would require reassessment.

Different specialists own different pieces. Engineers assess technical deliverability. Lawyers assess contractual rights. Accountants and finance teams assess financial statements and cash flows. Independent decision assurance asks whether those records, taken together, actually establish the proposition the capital allocator is relying on — and whether known gaps have been made explicit.

The productive-utilization debate is essential. So is the question of who will pay for AI intelligence, and at what price. But before authorizing the next investment, a chief financial officer or investment committee can ask an even more immediate question: Which assumptions are verified facts, which are binding commitments, which are conditional forecasts and which need to be revalidated because something material has changed?

A sector can produce transformational technology and disappointing returns on some of its assets. We have seen that distinction in markets before. The lesson is not to stop investing. It is to stop treating execution, delivery, utilization and durable economic value as if they were the same event.

On the trading floor, the confirmation of a trade was the beginning of understanding the position, not the end. AI infrastructure deserves the same discipline.

Neil P. Osnato is founder of Persistence Analytics Group LLC, an independent Decision Assurance™ and Infrastructure Assumption Verification™ firm. He previously spent 13 years trading on the New York Stock Exchange floor. His work focuses on the documentary evidence supporting consequential infrastructure and financial decisions. He wrote this article for SiliconANGLE.

Selected public background: MISO Large Load Working Group stakeholder feedback (LLWG-2026-7 and LLWG-2026-8), September 2026; The Bond Buyer, “Deciding About Data Centers,” October 28, 2026.
