AI Visibility Isn’t A Sham – Most Ways Of Measuring It Are via @sejournal, @gregjarboe

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

This webinar uses Conductor's research to help you prioritize marketing data and AI visibility measurement fixes.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Where search leaders are putting 2027 budget, plus the AI visibility scorecard that replaces session reporting.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Webinar: 9 AI Workflows For Better Content. Nov 18, 2 PM ET.

Make AI visibility budgets defensible with AMEC's data quality checks, tests against customer behavior, and brand measures like recall and consideration.

If an AI visibility dashboard helped you win budget this year, read four datasets before your next quarterly review. Together, they show AI assistants send a sliver of the traffic Google sends. They also show that none of these datasets could isolate the hidden AI influence many of us assumed was turning up in Direct and branded search.

That doesn’t make AI visibility a sham. It means most of the ways we measure it wouldn’t pass a basic data quality test.

On Oct. 22, 2026, Rand Fishkin published a 5-Minute Whiteboard on the SparkToro Blog titled “Is AI Visibility a Marketing Boondoggle?” The next day, Amanda Natividad, SparkToro’s chief evangelist, sent her Audience Research Newsletter asking, “What if AI visibility is a sham?”

A long time ago, I learned to separate events from trends. Two warnings from co-authors of the same book, Zero Click Marketing, who work at the same company and publish a day apart, look more like one event than two.

But the data underneath their warnings came from other people. And it has been piling up.

Marie Haynes looked at 12 months of GA4 data across 100 websites. ChatGPT visitors converted at 13.8%, against 3.02% for Google organic. That’s more than 4.5 times better, and it’s the stat AI visibility vendors will quote.

But here’s the stat they won’t. Google still drove 96.5% of total conversions in her dataset, and Google organic drove 27 times more conversions than all LLMs combined. She also found that the exponential growth in ChatGPT sessions ended in August 2025, around the release of GPT-5.

SEO Works found a narrower edge. AI referrals converted at 7.18% versus 6.08% for organic, a pooled ratio of 1.18. Yet AI traffic converted worse on 23 of the 63 sites with enough volume to compare. For B2B product businesses, AI converted at 0.62 times the organic rate.

The line from that study I’d tape to every SEO manager’s monitor is this one. A 5% lift in organic search delivered 10.3 additional conversions per site per month. Doubling AI referral traffic delivered 6.6.

The usual rebuttal is that clicks were never the point. People see a brand in a ChatGPT answer, don’t click, and then search for it or type in the URL later.

Kevin Indig went looking for that. At first, eight of his 12 properties showed Direct traffic growing from August to September. Then he filtered for engaged sessions. Across the filtered January-September sample, total Direct rose 140.9%, while engaged Direct changed by −1.0%. On one site, GA4 recorded 409,000 Direct sessions, and only 198 were engaged.

He then ran 632 tests on four brands, comparing weekly AI visibility with Direct traffic and branded search at delays of zero to six weeks. When he shifted the data to deliberately wrong dates as a placebo check, several of those mismatched alignments correlated as strongly as the real ones. His conclusion: “An effect could still exist. I just couldn’t isolate it.”

Natividad tied this back to an Ahrefs study of 75,000 brands from May 2025. Branded web mentions correlated at 0.664 with AI Overview brand visibility, and branded anchors at 0.527. Branded search volume came in last at 0.392. If AI visibility reliably produced branded search, she argued, that number should be much higher.

See also: Direct Traffic & Popularity – Correlation, Not Causation

Neither of them said AI doesn’t matter. Fishkin wrote that AI is “probably quite influential on some buyers in some sectors.” He credited Mike King with the better frame, that AI is “more like a brand channel, a digital billboard if you will, and it should be invested-in and measured as such.”

Natividad put it more sharply. “Familiarity itself is a marketing outcome.” Then she added the sentence that matters most for anyone building a dashboard. “If we can’t prove it, we shouldn’t trust the attribution just because it seems right.”

The SEO world is arriving late to a conversation the PR measurement world started months ago.

On May 20, 2026, AMEC, the International Association for the Measurement and Evaluation of Communication, launched its GEO Principles at its Global Summit in Dublin. Two days later, I admitted that I’d been treating AI citations as the new search rankings and that framing was wrong. Appearing in an AI answer is an output, whether it changes what people understand, believe, or do is an outcome.

AMEC has also gone a layer deeper with a companion white paper, “AMEC Data Quality Principles: Tackling the Unstructured Data Quality Challenges in the AI Era.” Its lead author is Rob Key, founder and CEO of Converseon, and it was produced with the Global Data Quality Initiative, a coalition that includes the Insights Association, ESOMAR, and the Market Research Society.

The paper was written for social listening and media analysts, not SEOs. Read it with an AI visibility dashboard open in another tab anyway. It proposes four questions any data set should pass before anyone acts on it.

The third question is the one that should keep SEO directors up at night. AMEC calls it predictive and construct validity. A metric can be “internally consistent but externally untrue,” the paper warns, and in an AI era that kind of data “does not stay quarantined; it propagates.”

That is a precise description of most AI visibility scores. They’re consistent from week to week. They go up when you publish more. Whether they predict anything a business cares about is, after Indig’s 632 tests, still unproven.

Two more lines from the paper belong in every GEO pitch deck. Principle four says, “Evaluations performed once and stored on a shelf are decorative, not protective.” And its crosswalk with AMEC’s GEO framework says outputs are directional and should be tested “across tools, prompts, and time.” A single screenshot of ChatGPT recommending your brand is an anecdote.

Principle five goes further. It tells practitioners to avoid manipulative GEO, meaning content engineered to game generative AI rather than inform it. AMEC’s phrase for it is “promotion over precision.”

One caution in the spirit of the paper itself. Some of its most striking figures, including the claim that off-the-shelf sentiment models hover at 60% to 65% precision, come from a Converseon-sponsored benchmark, and Converseon sells the cure. The paper calls itself non-promotional, and I think its principles stand on their own. But apply its Source question to the paper, too.

I don’t think AI visibility is a boondoggle. I think the AI visibility score is.

The datasets above don’t prove AI has no influence. They prove we’ve been grading a brand channel with traffic-channel math, then filling the gap with scores nobody has validated against behavior. That’s how a reasonable idea turns into a budget line you can’t defend.

Mike King’s billboard frame is the right one, and it fits what I’ve been arguing in my columns on YouGov’s brand research. AI brands are winning on consideration before they win on trust. If AI answers work like a billboard for your brand, measure them the way brand marketers have measured billboards for decades, with exposure, recall, and consideration, and stop asking them to behave like a search result.

Demote AI referral traffic to a diagnostic. Keep reporting it. Stop setting targets on it. Then run the SEO Works math on your own GA4 property. Calculate what a 5% lift in organic conversions is worth next to doubling your AI referrals. If organic wins, as it did across their sample, your roadmap should say so.

Run your dashboard through AMEC’s four questions. Before an AI visibility number goes into a deck, document which tools and prompts produced it, whether results were logged in or logged out, and how bot sessions were filtered. Define whether you’re counting a mention, a citation, or a recommendation, because they aren’t interchangeable. If you can’t answer those, the number fails AMEC’s Source and Accuracy questions before anyone asks about results.

Test your score for real-world validity, Indig style. Pull weekly AI visibility for your brand and line it up against engaged Direct sessions, branded search, and pipeline at lags of one to six weeks. Then shift the series to wrong dates and run it again. If the wrong dates correlate as well as the right ones, you’ve learned your score isn’t predicting anything yet. That’s worth knowing before your vendor renewal.

Build a governed prompt library and re-run it on a schedule. Pick prompts that map to real buyer questions, not prompts that flatter your brand. Run them across several AI tools, save the outputs, and repeat monthly or quarterly. AMEC’s principle is that accuracy isn’t a point in time, and the same holds for visibility.

Ask people, then triangulate. Indig’s recommendation is self-reported attribution. Add a free-text “How did you hear about us?” field to demo and signup forms, and add AI assistants to your brand lift surveys. Natividad warns that this data has its own flaws, so treat it as one input. Her framework of exposure, behavior, and business outcomes is a sensible way to weigh them together.

Put money where the evidence overlaps. SEO Works found the sites gaining AI referrals were the same sites improving in organic, with a correlation of +0.557. Ahrefs found branded web mentions tracked AI Overview visibility better than branded search did. Earned authority does double duty. That’s the least glamorous budget line on your plan and, right now, the best supported one.

Fishkin asked whether AI visibility is a marketing boondoggle. My answer is that it becomes one the moment we report a number we haven’t checked against what customers actually do.

Hera probe gets 1st good luck at asteroid system clobbered by NASA's DART mission | Space photo of the day for Nov. 6, 2026

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The European Space Agency's (ESA) Hera spacecraft has gotten its first close-up look at the Didymos binary asteroid system. If that system sounds familiar, it's because it made headlines in September 2022 when NASA's DART (Double Asteroid Redirection Test) spacecraft smashed into it.

The aim of the DART mission was to test whether an impact could divert an asteroid away from a collision course with Earth. The purpose of Hera is to follow up on DART by making up-close observations of the Didymos system.

"The first thing we said was, 'Wow,'" Hera Principal Investigator Patrick Michel said in a statement about the image, which ESA released on Thursday (Nov. 5). "This is the other side of Didymos from the one that DART’s camera showed us shortly before impact, so this feels like looking at a whole new world."

The Didymos system consists of the eponymous 2,560-foot-wide (780 meters) space rock and its "moonlet" Dimorphos, which is about 530 feet (160 m) in diameter.

The DART mission impacted Dimorphos on Sept. 26, 2022, with the aim of causing a change in the orbital period of the moonlet. And it worked: Before the impact, Dimorphos completed one lap around Didymos in around 11 hours and 55 minutes. The DART collision reduced the length of this period by around 33 minutes.

The change is a result of the fact that Dimporphos is now traveling at around 2.7 millimeters per second more slowly than it did before the collision.

The newly released photo shows Didymos in detail. Just a sliver of Dimorphos is visible, below and slightly to the left of the main asteroid.

The changes caused by DART were small. But in a real Earth-threatening scenario, even such a tiny shift could help divert an asteroid on a collision course — as long as scientists get enough advance warning of its approach.

Hera's observations should help reveal what other changes have occurred on and around Didymos.

Scientists are already seeing that, while Didymos has the same central ridge along its equator as before the impact, many changes have occurred. This includes the creation of multiple boulders, craters, and patterns of grooves or ridges.

"Since we expect a lot of impact-ejected material to have made it across the approximately 1 kilometer [0.62 miles] of space from Dimorphos to Didymos, it may be that some of these characteristics originate from this transport of material, and we have exciting work ahead of us to sort it out," said Michael Kueppers, ESA’s Hera mission scientist. "We look forward to seeing much more of both bodies and systemically mapping all the changes we identify."

Stay tuned to Space.com for more Hera findings from Didymos.

Robert Lea is a science journalist in the U.K. whose articles have been published in Physics World, New Scientist, Astronomy Magazine, All About Space, Newsweek and ZME Science. He also writes about science communication for Elsevier and the European Journal of Physics. Rob holds a bachelor of science degree in physics and astronomy from the U.K.’s Open University. Follow him on Twitter @sciencef1rst.

Quiz question: what was unique about Tony Cottee’s goal for West Ham against Charlton, in the FA Cup third round in January 1986? Answer: during that domestic season, it was the first live televised goal in either league or cup.

There hadn’t been loads of goalless draws before that – actually, there had been a complete TV blackout. No live football, not even Match of the Day highlights, after a dispute between the Football League and the broadcasters, BBC and ITV.

Even when tensions thawed, only 13 English club games were shown live during the second half of that campaign.

Fast forward 40 years, and you’d struggle to find a time where there weren’t at least 13 domestic league games televised in a single weekend. This season, a minimum of 267 Premier League matches will be shown live on Sky and TNT, plus 1,000 games from the EFL, League Cup and EFL Trophy, with the FA Cup and European games on top of that.

That’s just one example of how football has changed over the years – new state-of-the-art arenas have also been built around the country.

But as football has got bigger, what price has it all come at? Political interference, financial cheating and the quest to get every refereeing decision right to within a millimetre, leading to moments of spontaneous joy being sucked away from those in the stands.

In this issue, we ask if football is broken – and if so, how do we fix it?

Anker Soundcore Space 2 Pro review: Big bang for less bucks

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The company’s latest headphones rival Apple, Bose and Sony at a much lower price.

We may receive a commission on purchases made from links.

If you don't like the idea of paying $350 to $500 for a set of flagship headphones, Anker's Soundcore line offers a lot of performance at much lower prices. Over the years, the company has refined its formula by balancing sound and features in various packages so as to compete with the likes of Apple, Bose, Sony and Sennheiser. With the Space 2 Pro, Anker put its new Thus audio chip in headphones for the first time, making lofty claims about the upgraded voice clarity as a result. At $200, these aren't just an affordable alternative to the big names in headphones, they're a worthy contender in their own right.

The first thing that stood out to me about the Space 2 Pro is the design. These do not look like they cost $200, especially not the grey version that I tested. Anker refined the look of the Space line here for a premium aesthetic and a build that's lightweight, but not overly plastic. The sliders for fit adjustments are metal and both the headband and ear pads are wrapped in soft leather. There are also subtle metallic accents to complete the premium design, like the circles on the outside of the ear cups and subtly placed product name.

Even though the Space 2 Pro is the flagship Soundcore headphones, the price tag pits them up against decidedly midrange competition. Design-wise, the Space 2 Pro is vastly superior in that category, and that advantage continues into audio performance. These headphones have more bass and overall clarity than you'll typically find in the $200 range. The 35mm drivers are made with two different materials in order to maximize both high-end clarity and deep bass.

When I listened to Mastodon's Marrow Deep, Brann Dailor's drums boom throughout and Troy Sanders' bass provides the backbone for the guitar riffs. Thanks to Anker's "crystalline highs" here, guitars and synths cut through the mix clearly, so you never miss a note of Bill Kelliher's handiwork. The overall clarity and detail on the Space 2 Pro is really on display with more acoustic-driven genres, like Zach Bryan's Young Manhood and Other Donatable Organs. The piano, acoustic guitar and string section seem to float behind the interwoven vocals of Bryan and Lana Del Rey on "Lies," for example.

Anker is very proud that the Space 2 Pro currently holds the Guiness World Record for the "clearest over-ear headphones for calls." I was immediately skeptical that this would hold any weight in real-world use, but boy was I wrong.

Plenty of headphones manage to nullify background noise, but they do so at great expense to voice clarity. But thanks to that Thus chip and a six-microphone array, the Space 2 Pro offers extremely clear voice performance while effectively blocking background noise. Not only do they combat would-be distractions exceptionally well, they do so without making you sound even the slightest bit robotic. I thought I sounded pretty close to how I did in a quiet room when the processing kicked in, maybe only the slightest bit muffled, but never like a digitized version of myself.

The company promises battery life of up to 30 hours when you have active noise cancellation (ANC) on, or up to 60 hours with it off. During my testing, which I conducted almost entirely with noise cancellation enabled, I got 31 hours before the Space 2 Pro shut off. I did take a call or two a day where I needed to switch to transparency mode so I didn't feel the need to shout to hear myself speaking, but I'd say 95 percent of the time, I was using ANC.

The most disappointing aspect of the Space 2 Pro to me are a number of subpar audio features. The Thus chip powers what Anker calls Hear ID 5.0, a feature that uses a hearing test to customize the sound of the headphones to your preferences. I didn't think the resulting tailored audio profile offered any meaningful improvement over the stock tuning, neither did spatial audio settings or the collection of curated presets. I tested all of them, of course, but every time I used one, I found myself longing for the out-of-the-box sound after just a few minutes of use.

Anker boasts Adaptive ANC 3.0 on the Space 2 Pro, a noise-canceling setup that relies on the Thus chip and eight microphones to combat background noise. The company specifically lists "blocking office chatter and keyboard clicks for instant silence" among the headphones' strengths. While I do think the Space 2 Pro does an above average job of lowering the volume on human voices, and it definitely blocks keyboard clicks, its general noise canceling abilities are just okay. The headphones struggled with constant noise sources, like white noise machines and particularly raucous fans.

On Anker's flagship earbuds, the Liberty 5 Pro Max, the company built an AI note taking assistant into the charging case (which has a touchscreen). Even though the Space 2 Pro doesn't have a case, it does have plenty of microphones, and "AI Note-Taker" is currently listed as "TBD" on the spec sheet. If that feature does eventually arrive, there are two potential issues with it.

First, it will be a lot more awkward to wear over-ear headphones in a meeting than it would be to slide a small earbuds case to the middle of the table. Second, if you plan to regularly use Anker's transcription service and cloud storage, you'll have to pay for a subscription. You only get two hours of transcriptions and 10,000 minutes of audio storage monthly for free, and only for two years. Plans are then either $8.33 or $20 per month (billed annually) depending on your needs. Since it's unclear if this feature will ever arrive, its absence doesn't impact my overall assessment.

It's easy to dismiss Soundcore as merely a budget brand, but that would be a mistake. With products like the Space 2 Pro, Anker proves it can hang with the best, offering comparable performance for significantly less money. In fact, I'd argue if voice clarity is your primary factor in choosing a new set of headphones, this model should be your first choice. For many, though, that's further down the list of priorities.

While there are caveats when it comes to ANC performance, audio features and a (potentially) unnecessary note taker, the Space 2 Pro has a lot of premium traits. The overall sound quality is good, the design is refined, the fit is comfortable and battery life claims hold true. At a time when the best overall headphones you can buy are $460 at full price, Anker's ability to produce a $200 alternative that won't disappoint you is no small feat.

The Verge’s 2026 holiday gift guide

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Our latest holiday cheat sheet is jam-packed with our favorite tech, along with Play-Doh, vinyl, and plenty of analog picks.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Gift-giving isn’t just about grabbing something solid under a determined spending limit. It’s an expression of love, a way to show someone how well you really know them. That doesn’t mean you have to do it all on your own, though. Sometimes you just need a push in the right direction to get things rolling.

This year, we spent hours collecting our favorites to help you get ahead of your holiday shopping. This list has everything: minimalist smart rings, Play-Doh flowers, sleek beat machines — you name it. Whatever your needs, no matter how demanding the recipient, you’ll find something exceptional below for that special someone on your list.

Most of us have thousands of photos in the cloud, but if a photo never makes it into the real world, did it really happen? Existentialism aside, the Cricut StickerPix offers a new way to play with photos, providing you with everything you need to turn any picture into a pro-quality print or sticker.

If you’re a fan of Apple’s open-ear standard AirPods, the AirPods 5 are the best model yet. The higher-end pair (there are two models) offers upgraded sound, better noise cancellation, and swipe-based volume controls, bringing some Pro-level features to the buds that started the wire-free revolution.

It’s rare for a product to boast that it’s “hacker-friendly,” but the Busy Bar isn’t your average device. Equipped with a pixelated LED screen, fantastically fun buttons, and intriguing ways to play, Flipper’s open-source productivity tool breeds efficiency by, among other things, keeping coworkers from interrupting your flow with their weekend plans.

Espresso in the woods? Yes, please. The GR2’s magical mechanics make it possible, letting you turn 12 ounces of grounds into espresso without power. Its carrying case houses all the accessories needed to get that sweet shot of bean, making real coffee available virtually anywhere.

Digital photos changed everything, but there’s still something wonderful about the visceral touch and nostalgia of analog. Fujifilm’s Instax Mini Evo provides an intriguing hybrid that bridges these worlds, with a retro design, intuitive dials and controls, and the ability to filter like it’s 1989 (or 1889?).

What if you could give the gift of stress-free Wi-Fi on the go? That’s the promise of TP-Link’s dual-band Roam 7, a portable Wi-Fi 7 router that simplifies connecting to public portals so travelers can connect everything to a single device and be done with it.

For those who love travel but hate the constraints of a hotel TV, Belkin’s ConnectAir is a slick solution. Plug the transmitter into any device with a USB-C video-out port and the receiver into the TV’s HDMI port, and voilà, you’re easily (and wirelessly) connected.

The company that brought gaming to the masses still defies the odds, thanks in large part to its creative roots. Those roots run deep, and journalist Keza MacDonald’s fascinating book traces them to their source, from a simple card game company to the minds behind the biggest hits of all time.

You can give the gift of creativity with this do-it-all digital music maker. Akai’s portable sampler, beat maker, and all-around sound machine blends tactile quick keys and pads with an onboard screen so musicians can build it all in a box and send the results to their DAW of choice.

For those who enjoy clever devices that just work, we give you Nimble’s “breakable” SharePower Portable Charger. The magnetically joined power bank quickly snaps into a pair of 20W batteries, letting two people charge their phones, or works as a single 10,000mAh unit for larger top-offs.

New Zealand artist Aldous Harding has another eccentric album on her hands with this year’s Train on the Island. The slow-burning LP is gorgeously recorded and delightfully weird, and it feels tailor-made for vinyl, making it an easy pick for Harding fans (or anyone who loves quirky indie jams).

For those in smaller dwellings, efficient living means maximizing space. That’s where combo appliances like Ninja’s newest microwave / air fryer combo come in. The handy countertop gadget lets you zap your food, then crisp it at the end so your pizza or French fries don’t turn into a soggy mess.

Perhaps no game in history is more anticipated than the latest entry in Rockstar’s sprawling, intricate, and absolutely bonkers open-world series. Grand Theft Auto VI is said to offer a more obsessively dense map, more realism in details like weather and passing time, and more realistic NPCs (non-player characters) than ever before. Here’s hoping those 13 years were worth the wait.

Open-style earbuds aren’t just for the sporty; they’re an entirely different way to listen that keeps you environmentally aware without the plugged-up feeling of traditional buds. The OpenFit Pro take a balanced approach, with seamless open-ear performance and even a touch of noise reduction to dampen the din on crowded commutes.

The old Ironman Timex was a staple for ’80s and ’90s kids, and while you can’t bring back those carefree days, remakes like the Retroware can bring back that carefree feeling. No smarts, no notifications, just a takes-a-licking Timex watch with tactile keys, 50 meters of water resistance, and totally tubular digital graphics.

Serious gamers need a serious controller, and few take gaming more seriously than 8BitDo’s Elite-style Xbox gamepad. From its drift-proof TMR joysticks and motion support to its customizable buttons and remappable bumpers, the Ultimate 3E is the gift for that lovable control freak in your life.

There’s regular Play-Doh for kids, but did you know that Play-Doh makes kits for adults, too? We’re not scoffing. The new Blooms sets take on Lego’s floral bouquets with more realistic-looking flower arrangements, and they include a vase. They’re equal parts nostalgic kitsch and stylish decor.

For on-the-go content creators, it’s all about capturing the moment in the highest video quality possible. Insta360’s Luna Pro steadycam offers plenty of features to get the job done right, including 4K vertical video, swift file transfers, four hours of battery life, and a detachable touchscreen that conveniently doubles as a remote.

The new Fitbit Air provides the same great fitness tracking we’ve come to expect in a comfortable design that virtually disappears on your wrist. Google’s AI Coach can be overly assertive, but the tracker’s great battery life and metrics for everything from sleep to blood oxygen make it an excellent gift for anyone who needs a healthy hand.

Most mobile controllers have a serious size limit. Not Razer’s Kishi V3 Pro XL, which lets you throw a big ol’ tablet between the handles for a major visual upgrade. It also has high-quality joysticks, great grips, and customizable keys for USB-C tablets, along with haptics for Android models.

So what if you’ve heard “Golden” more times than you can count? Netflix’s massive animated hit is a lot easier to gift now that it’s officially available on 4K Blu-ray disc. The story about three pop stars who band together to save humanity from a demonic boy band is an instant classic, with fantastic music and striking animation.

If you’re contemplating a pricey pair of sleep buds for your loved one, we urge you to halt and consider a softer (and cheaper) alternative. Piercing pillows, designed with pockets for freshly pierced ears, also work great for regular earbuds. Blissbury’s comfy and adjustable Ear Pillow is our favorite. Problem — and sleep issues — solved.

The Concrete Edition of Keychron’s K2 HE is not your average mechanical board. Cased in actual concrete, it offers standard keycaps to match its industrial aesthetic, both Bluetooth and 2.4GHz connectivity, and several other advanced features. This includes the ability to adjust the actuation distance of the individual Hall effect switches (hence the “HE”).

We all do it: pay a ton for a monthly streaming service for just a few series. It’s better to own the real thing, and Hacks, a hilarious drama that follows a comedian (Jean Smart) and her young writer (Hannah Einbinder) in the tumultuous “biz,” is one of HBO’s best series in years.

Hoto’s soldering iron is super cool — and by that we mean hot, as it’s able to reach nearly 400 degrees Fahrenheit in about three seconds. The cordless, adjustable iron makes the perfect gift for your favorite DIYer, especially since it features customizable presets for virtually any job.

For the person on your list whose hatred for smart glasses runs deep, Knockaround’s hip, tech-free sunglasses should hit the spot. They’re available in a variety of fun styles and offer lightweight frames and polarized lenses, with both UVA and UVB shielding for attractive eye protection.

Pokopia brings Pokémon to life in a cozy worldbuilding game that borrows the best from predecessors like Minecraft and Animal Crossing. The twist? The slow-burning Switch 2 title eventually rolls into an expansive adventure that lets you customize as you go.

When it comes to keeping your pet happy and healthy while you’re away, the details matter. Petkit’s most basic auto feeder offers peace of mind with dual-power protection, meal monitoring via the Petkit app, and a generous 12-cup capacity that should keep an adult cat fed for up to 15 days.

Electronics are becoming more sustainable, and Fairphone is leading the way. The Fairphone 6 Plus may seem like a regular Android phone on paper, but the ability to repair and replace its internals, from the screen to the battery, makes it anything but average.

In many ways, the Pebble Index 01 is the gadget of our times. The stripped-back smart ring has a microphone and, with a tap of a button, records and sends your thoughts to the Pebble app over Bluetooth. You can also use it to start a timer or even add a calendar event to your phone.

ChatGPT's New GIF Generator Is Fun, but Won't Replace Us Humans (Yet)

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Khamosh Pathak is a freelance tech journalist with over 13 years of experience writing online. An accounting graduate, he turned his interest in writing and technology into a career. He holds a specialization in user experience design from ISDI Parsons in Mumbai, where he sought to deepen his understanding of UX, consumer devices, and interplay between software and technology.

Khamosh has written thousands of how-tos and guides on every major platform (iOS, macOS, Android, and Windows). He got started writing how-tos and guides for Guiding Tech, where he also published several e-books. His work has also been seen at MakeUseOf, How-To Geek, and PCMag. He is truly passionate about brewing good coffee.

When it comes to generating AI images, both Gemini and ChatGPT do a passable job. In most cases, the results will look AI-generated, but they likely won’t have glaring spelling issues or missing limbs. Now, OpenAI is trying something new, introducing GIF support for ChatGPT. It's the company's first "moving images" feature since OpenAI shut down Sora, and while you can generate hyper-realistic videos in Gemini, this new ChatGPT feature is trying to fill in the gap, in a small way.

OpenAI is pitching the new feature as an animation tool, not exactly as a way to create looping videos—though that’s how I ended up using it. You give ChatGPT a prompt, as detailed as you can make it, and the chatbot produces a GIF in just a few seconds. You can start with your own drawings or images, too. ChatGPT uses sketches as the base for animations, though it can turn any still image into animated GIFs. Like other AI editing tools, you can edit it if ChatGPT didn't quite get it right: You can ask ChatGPT to make the GIF longer or shorter, remove certain objects, change the style, slow down transitions, among other edits.

AI art can be quite sterile, but I was surprised by how adding motion and animation to the mix makes generating images a lot more fun. Unlike traditional GIFs, which usually pull from existing video clips, you can turn anything you want into a GIF, which is especially cool if you're using your own art. In my testing, I found this to be the most rewarding part. It’s not always possible to one-shot a GIF with a single prompt. But after a few rounds of edits, I ended up creating some GIFs that I actually wanted to share in my WhatsApp groups.

That being said, I think human-made GIFs are still the gold standard here. For example, I asked ChatGPT to generate a video of someone brewing a pour-over, with a gooseneck kettle, showing the coffee blooming process. It did okay, but it’s nothing like actually making a pour-over. The kettle doesn’t move in circles, the water doesn’t rise evenly, there are no bubbles, and the steam is all over the place.

Compare that to the GIF below, which comes from a royalty-free video. Even though it’s a compressed, low-quality, and noisy GIF, it still shows the movement of the pour, the water rising, and even the bubbles. The details captured by a real video just aren’t there in ChatGPT GIFs. If that’s what you’re expecting, you will be disappointed. But I don't think that's the point of this feature anyway.

I found that ChatGPT excels when you’re making something fun, and you don’t look into the details too much. It pulled off a loop of a cat playing with a ball, and it was great at making a festive GIF for Diwali. It even made a GIF based on a photo. I asked it to create a fun promotional GIF for my JBL Bluetooth speakers, and it did the job quite well. At first, the transitions were too jarring, and the movement just made no sense. After some editing, though, I got it to a decent place.

Still, the main issue with ChatGPT GIFs is that complex movement and transitions aren’t nailed down. A hand shifts position weirdly between frames, and colors and objects appear out of nowhere. But if you can get over these issues, you can make some fun, lighthearted GIFs and memes to share with your friends on Instagram or WhatsApp.

Aave Labs buys Avon team in fixed-rate lending push

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Aave has more than $30 billion in deposits and over $12 billion in active loans, according to the company.

Aave Labs has acquired the team and technology behind Avon as it works to add fixed-rate lending to its onchain lending network.

The acquisition includes Avon's fixed- and floating-rate lending protocols, an onchain order book, domains and related assets, according to a statement shared with The Block on Friday. Financial terms were not disclosed.

As part of the deal, Prince Sinha and Ayush Kaul will join Aave Labs' engineering team, while Omar el-Ashaal will join its business development team as an associate.

"Aave’s goal is to bring the best of finance to everyone, and that means offering the kinds of products users already rely on, including fixed rates," Aave Labs CEO Stani Kulechov said. "The Avon team have spent time working on some of the hardest problems in onchain lending, and they will help us bring Aave to institutions, fintechs, and everyday users around the world."

Avon was founded by Sinha and developed lending markets focused on fixed and floating rates, along with an order book designed for new onchain lending structures. Other developments by the company include interest-rate pricing, risk isolation, and lending market design.

Aave said the Avon team will initially work on fixed-rate lending as the network expands the types of loans available to users.

Aave is the largest onchain lending network, according to the statement, with more than $30 billion in deposits and over $12 billion in active loans.

Sovereign AI: What Changes When The Cloud Is No Longer Enough

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Karan Alang, principal software engineer at Versa Networks with 25 years of experience in AI, cloud and big data.

For most companies, cloud computing is a sensible way to start developing AI systems. On-demand resources and managed services make it easier to build data pipelines, try use cases and scale as adoption increases.

However, public clouds don’t always satisfy the legal, operational and security requirements of a sovereign environment.

Gartner defines sovereignty as an organization’s ability to control its data, technology and digital operations. It’s “about who operates and supports your IT systems.” This includes control over provider operations, personnel and access paths, not just where the data is located.​

This is becoming increasingly important in the AI/ML age, especially for regulated and security-sensitive organizations. Financial institutions face privacy, record-keeping and jurisdictional requirements; healthcare systems must protect sensitive workloads, and government agencies may require FedRAMP-authorized or fully air-gapped environments​.

AI makes this complex because data is no longer confined to traditional databases and applications. It can flow through training pipelines, vector databases and inference systems. With agentic AI, autonomous agents may also access and act on it. Organizations therefore need to understand not only where the data resides but where it’s processed, and what systems and agents access it.​​

Sovereignty can be achieved on the cloud as well as an air-gapped on-premise environment. A sovereign cloud environment provides a middle ground between using a cloud-based environment and an air-gapped sovereign environment. The comparison below shows how sovereign cloud and on-premise environments satisfy sovereignty.​

• Sovereign Cloud: High, depending on the provider and region. Data can be constrained to an approved country or jurisdiction, with regional storage or processing and controls over data movement.

• On-Premise: Very high. Data is stored within the organization’s air-gapped controlled environment.

• Sovereign Cloud: Partial to high. The customer has significant controls, but the cloud provider still operates parts of the underlying infrastructure. Support personnel, privileged access, telemetry and management-plane dependencies need be evaluated.

• On-Premise: Very high. The customer controls administrators, physical infrastructure, networking, patching, deployment, monitoring, keys and access.

• Sovereign Cloud: Partial. The customer generally remains dependent on the provider’s hardware, cloud platform, APIs, managed services and service roadmap. For example, GPU availability on the cloud might be restricted to specific regions.

• On-Premise: Potentially very high, but not absolute. The customer selects and operates its compute, storage, networking, Kubernetes/platform stack, models and inference environment. However, dependencies can remain on GPU vendors, commercial software, firmware and externally developed models.​

The sovereign cloud provides on-demand provisioning while satisfying the sovereignty requirement of many organizations. But for highly sensitive data and AI workloads, some organizations require stronger isolation and operational controls provided by air-gapped environments.

Air-gapped sovereign AI has traditionally been cost-prohibitive and difficult to manage. However, technological changes have made air-gapped AI more practical.

• AI compute is increasingly accessible outside hyperscale data centers. Enterprise servers with modern GPUs now support meaningful inference workloads inside a customer-controlled environment.

• Local LLM inference has matured. Open-weight models can run on customer hardware without organizations having to send sensitive data to an external model API. Additionally, quantization techniques help reduce model size to run efficiently on enterprise server GPUs.

• The open-source infrastructure stack is production-ready and portable. Technologies like Kubernetes, Kafka, Airflow and Spark run across cloud and on-premise environments, bringing cloud-native principles to sovereign environments.

I’ve spent the last few years building cloud-native data platforms for network and security intelligence. We started with the cloud, which was the right starting point. It let us move quickly, iterate and scale. However, for some customers, an air-gapped sovereign AI solution is the correct one.

Moving the platform into an air-gapped environment taught us that sovereign AI isn’t simply cloud architecture deployed on-premises. The operational considerations and requirements change significantly.​

There are several key points to be considered while formulating an enterprise AI strategy and in the design/implementation of an air-gapped sovereign AI solution.

• Capacity is now finite. Every microservice and Kubernetes workload must be right-sized upfront—CPU limits, memory requests, persistent and ephemeral storage, and GPU allocation. The elasticity of the cloud isn’t available in an air-gapped on-premise environment; hence, the size needs to account for the peak loads.

• The upfront infrastructure cost may be significant and needs to be calculated appropriately. The lag between the hardware procurement and delivery must be factored into capacity planning.

• Replacing the managed services and their capabilities needs additional engineering effort and planning

• Observability is a key part of the design. The pre-built metrics visualizations for managed services like Composer, Mongo Atlas and Dataproc needs to be recreated.

• Log aggregation needs to be built using technologies like Grafana and Loki, This is critical for observability and compliance.

• CI/CD must be redesigned for the air-gapped environment. Internal artifact registries need to be sized and set up to securely store and distribute docker images and other software artifacts.

• Support teams need to be equipped to handle infrastructure and product issues. The cost estimate needs to include 24/7 support.

• Model and hardware selection must match the workload. NetOps and SecOps environments combine high-volume structured telemetry, real-time processing and language-based investigation. Model size, quantization, inference throughput and GPU memory all affect the architecture—and in an air-gapped deployment, getting those choices correct is critical.

As AI becomes embedded in organizations, sovereignty needs to be part of the architecture design considerations from the beginning. For many organizations, a sovereign cloud environmemt provides the right balance of control and flexibility. For highly sensitive or mission-critical workloads, an air-gapped infrastructure may be more appropriate.

The technological advancements in AI models and Infrastructure now makes these deployments increasingly practical, but greater control also means greater responsibility. Capacity, observability, security, software distribution and operational support need to be incorporated into the platform design considerations.

That’s the key lesson of sovereign AI: Organizations should design for sovereignty from day one rather than trying to retrofit it later.​

Nothing will ship its first AI device in December, and it’s built for work

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Chief executive Carl Pei shared only a dark teaser image of the product. He argues that AI will win over offices before homes, and that its biggest gap is context.

Nothing will ship its first AI device in December, and it is built for work. Chief executive Carl Pei announced it on Wednesday in a long essay on X. He gave no name, price or launch date.

“It’s a tool, but one built with the personality and care that have come to define a Nothing product. It has no new screen, and it couldn’t have been an app,” Pei wrote.

His only image shows the dark corner of a rounded device. It carries the words “Nothing (R)” and “Coming soon”.

Most of Pei’s essay argued against the hunt for a single breakthrough AI gadget.

“The whole industry is hunting for its iPhone moment: the pendant, the pin, the glasses, the companion, some magical consumer device that makes AI part of everyone’s life,” he wrote. “I admire the boldness, but I read this moment differently.”

Pei said new technology wins people over at work first. Computers spread as spreadsheet machines and mobile phones as car phones for dealmakers, he wrote. He said coding alone makes up more than half of what companies spend on AI built for specific jobs.

Pei wants to fix what he calls a “context gap”. AI models are capable, he wrote, but they know little about a person’s day. People still copy emails, calendars and Slack threads into a chat box, and they lose ideas from conversations.

“Close it, and everything AI can already do gets dramatically more useful,” Pei wrote.

Glean chief executive Arvind Jain also talked about giving AI more context about work on the TNW podcast in October.

Pei also argued that work tools should look as good as consumer ones, and pointed to Olivetti’s Valentine typewriter. Nothing makes phones and earbuds, and Pei wrote that “tens of millions” of its devices are in use.

Nothing first announced plans for “AI-native” devices about a year ago, The Verge noted. A report last month said it plans two pairs of AI smart glasses. Rivals already sell AI glasses, from Alibaba’s Qwen N1 to Meta’s Ray-Bans.

New evidence for ancient cannabis use

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

Jonathan Wosen is STAT’s West Coast biotech & life sciences reporter. You can reach Jonathan on Signal at jwosen.27.

Theresa covers substance use and addiction and co-writes the Morning Rounds newsletter. You can reach Theresa on Signal at theresagaff.97.

Get your daily dose of health and medicine every weekday with STAT’s free newsletter Morning Rounds. Sign up here.

Congratulations on making it to the end of the week. This is Jonathan Wosen, STAT’s West Coast Biotech & Life Sciences Reporter. We’ve got an eclectic lineup for you today, including a look at how declining vitamin K shots are impacting infants, why medical records often don’t tell the full story about women’s health, and results on health and science-related ballot measures across the country.

Oh, and a study that unearths evidence of cannabis use from a 2,800-year-old Siberian tomb (pun intended). Let’s dig in.

That’s how much higher the risk of brain bleeding during the first six months of life was in infants who didn’t receive a vitamin K shot, compared to those who did. Babies are born with low levels of vitamin K, which plays an essential role in forming blood clots. That’s why the American Academy of Pediatrics has for more than 60 years recommended that newborns get the one-time shot shortly after birth. But medical mistrust has led a growing number of parents to turn down the injection; the share of newborns who weren’t given vitamin K rose from 3.1% in 2017 to 6.8% in 2025.

That trend has had grim consequences, according to a study published Thursday in the Journal of the American Medical Association. Researchers looked back on data from more than 5 million infants born between 2017 and 2025 and found that, compared to infants given vitamin K, those who weren’t given K had a 47% higher risk of bleeding during their first six months and a 169% higher risk of dying while bleeding shortly after birth.

In recent years, the Kratom plant and related synthetic products have become omnipresent across smoke shops and gas station shelves, buoyed by proponents who extol these products’ pain-relieving powers. But public health experts have raised serious concerns about the potential dangers of kratom — and the fact that these products are not closely regulated.

It’s a debate that is confusing to many; two-thirds of Americans have never even heard of kratom. STAT’s Theresa Gaffney has you covered with this timely explainer on the drug. The piece covers how a plant that has been chewed and brewed for centuries in Southeast Asia due to its stimulant-like effects has quickly risen in popularity in the U.S., where some users say kratom has eased chronic pain and helped them get off opioids.

Theresa also digs into 7-OH and MGPI, semi-synthetic related products that some experts worry are especially addictive. Clinicians who run addiction treatment centers say that they’ve seen a rise in patients seeking help to get off 7-OH in particular. But for now, data on overdoses and deaths are murky.

You might think that your medical records are an objective account of your health. But they’re not, write epidemiologist Annemarie Hirsch and sociologist Jennifer Silva. The pair recently published a study in which they paired electronic medical records and interviews with 87 women about pain, illness, and encounters with the health care system.

Many of the women described trust-breaking encounters with health care providers and misdiagnoses, but these moments were rarely documented. The authors point to the story of a woman who lived with debilitating pelvic pain for more than a decade as a poignant example. Doctors at first dismissed her pain as related to obesity, pivoting to mental health after she lost weight. Notably, the turning point only came after her husband joined her for an appointment, and she was ultimately diagnosed with endometriosis. Her medical records chronicle this diagnostic odyssey, but they don’t capture the frustration and disillusionment she experienced after years of having her pain downplayed. Hirsch and Silva write that such stories raise concerns about the use of AI to advance women’s health.

“These tools are only as good as the data in the medical record,” they write. “If a woman’s symptoms are filtered through assumptions about her weight or mental health, and the true story of her pain is omitted, AI may learn and reproduce how the woman is framed in the record.”

Many Democrats are still celebrating the blue wave that handed their party control of both the House and Senate. But that momentum didn’t fully translate to health-related ballot initiatives across the country. As of late Thursday, voters in Arizona, Colorado, and Washington had approved or were on track to endorse measures that would prohibit transgender youth from participating on school sports teams aligned with their gender identity. Voters in four states with abortion-related ballot measures chose to preserve the status quo, with Missouri, Nevada, and Virginia choosing to maintain abortion rights while Idaho preserved the state’s existing ban.

Meanwhile, California voters appear on track to endorse Proposition 38, which would authorize $8.4 billion in bonds to fund immunology research. The measure has sparked controversy because its main backer is also the co-founder of an institute poised to receive half the funds. South Dakota voters rejected an amendment that would have conditioned a state Medicaid expansion on federal financial support holding steady. And Massachusetts voters can breathe easier (well, sort of) after a measure to repeal the state’s recreational marijuana law was defeated handily.

You can read more from me and Theresa Gaffney. But first, let’s stick with weed for a moment longer.

Early writing by the ancient Greek historian Herodotus — known for his foundational text on the Greco-Persian wars and banger quotes like, “of all possessions a friend is the most precious” — is a key source used by historians to support the idea that prehistoric communities were smoking weed. Archaeological evidence is hard to come by (plants are perishable!) and just as tricky to interpret. But a new paper, published yesterday in Science, offers compelling evidence of ritual cannabis use in a 2,800-year-old Siberian tomb.

The monumental burial mound included a log cabin-like structure, one coffin, and the remains of multiple individuals and horse sacrifices. There, archaeologists found hundreds of cannabis seeds, a handful of which were analyzed and dated to the first decade of the eighth century BCE. But there was an even rarer finding: two long braids of human hair containing significant amounts of the psychoactive compound cannabinol. “Although proving intent in a prehistoric context will remain impossible,” the authors wrote, “the evidence is strongly in favor of intentional consumption for the plant’s psychoactive properties.”

Admittedly, this is not our usual fare. But the study is a good reminder that drug use is not a modern issue, but rather a phenomenon that’s been with us for millenia. — Theresa Gaffney

Child dies of measles in Wisconsin’s first such death since 1990, Associated Press

Where To Hide Secondary Name In WoW: Forever

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

World of Warcraft: Forever introduces a two-part naming system, giving each character a main name and a secondary name. Rather than being unique to an individual realm, your full name must now be unique across your region.

This means you can still use a main name another player has already chosen, as long as the full two-part combination is available. But maybe you picked a random secondary name because you couldn't wait to see how your favorite starting area has changed since 2004. Luckily, you can control how that secondary name appears in-game.

Your secondary name appears above your character's head, in your character's portrait frame, and above your pet or minion. You can hide it from these UI elements if you'd prefer to see only your main name.

After unchecking the box, your secondary name will disappear from the affected displays immediately, without needing to restart the game. If you change your mind, return to the same menu and check the box again to restore it.

However, keep a couple of things in mind.

Your full character name will still appear in chat, even with the setting disabled. The setting also carries over between characters and rulesets. If you want to hide your secondary name on one character but show it on another, you'll need to change the setting each time you swap between your characters.

Pirate Software’s OnlyFangs raid controversy just got put on blast in a new WoW: Forever trailer.

In realm-based versions of World of Warcraft, players could use the same character name on different realms because names only needed to be unique within each realm. The new realmless structure removes that separation, making secondary names necessary to distinguish players who choose the same main name.

Think of secondary names like surnames. Two characters can share a main name as long as their full names differ. This flexibility is especially useful because numbers and symbols aren't supported in character names. Instead of changing the spelling of your favorite main name, you can pair it with a different secondary name. Keep in mind that Blizzard's standard naming rules still apply to both parts.

Whether you choose a meaningful surname or something random to get started, you don't have to keep it on display. Your full name remains intact, but how much of it you see is up to you.

Whether you're looking for skill-building, combat, questing, or more, we've got some suggestions for the best MMOs in 2026.

Iman Vellani wants everyone to know why X-Men: The Last Stand "failed"

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Iman Vellani voices her displeasure over the third X-Men movie

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Ms. Marvel actor Iman Vellani has explained why the notorious comic book flop X-Men: The Last Stand "failed" and should have been a trilogy.

Speaking on the Countdown to Avengers: Doomsday Official Podcast that highlighted Fox's X-Men films – a key text on the road to Doomsday – Vellani first took aim at The Last Stand disposing of James Marsden's Scott Summers, before moving on to the treatment of Jean Grey and the adaptation of the Phoenix Saga storyline as a whole.

"He's integral to Jean's story in the Dark Phoenix Saga. To get rid of him in the first 10 minutes completely eliminates any emotional resonance this movie could have had," Vellani said.

If you're familiar with Vellani's Letterboxd account, you'll know she openly isn't a fan of all of Fox's X-Men movies. The Last Stand received a 2.5 score alongside X-Men 2 (ouch), with 2000's X-Men faring slightly better at 3.5.

Much of Vellani's dislike for The Last Stand is how it handles the iconic Dark Phoenix Saga arc. In Marvel's comics, Jean Grey was slowly – and tragically – transformed by the Phoenix Force, an entity that could have wiped out the entire universe. In truth, The Last Stand's abridged version didn't do it justice.

"It's so reductive of that entire storyline," Vellani said. "If they wanted to do the Phoenix Saga properly, it should have been three or four movies, even, and really milk it. There's so much that happens – it's so thrilling, cosmic, and expansive. To stuff it into one movie with 'The Cure' storyline, oh my god. This movie failed the minute they made Jean's story also secondary."

Golden Bachelorette’s Joan Vassos, Chock Chapple Break Up

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Golden Bachelorette stars Joan Vassos and Chock Chappel have broken up, calling off their engagement two years after they met on the inaugural season of the Bachelor Nation franchise.

Joan Vassos and Chock Chapple, who met on the inaugural season of The Golden Bachelorette have broken up, calling off their engagement after two years together.

"Two years ago, Chock and I took a leap of faith and got engaged during our season of The Golden Bachelorette, hoping we had found our forever," a statement shared to Vassos' Instagram Stories Nov. 6 read. "We spent the last two years living what we hoped would be the beginning of our love story, sharing our families, amazing adventures, laughter and supporting each other through challenging times."

However, as she and Chapple—who also shared the same statement to his account—noted, "Sadly, our journey hasn't turned out the way we had hoped and we have decided to end our engagement."

"We truly want each other to be happy," she concluded, "and I'm grateful for the chapter we shared."

Vassos first raised eyebrows about her and Chapple’s relationship status when she stepped out at the Page Six Virtual Reali-Tea Awards Oct. 6 without her engagement ring.

Following Chapple’s proposal to Vassos in the July 2024 finale of The Golden Bachelorette, the couple had a slow and steady approach to planning their nuptials, previously teasing they’d be open to a televised ceremony like The Golden Bachelor’s Gerry Turner and Theresa Nist opted for. (The inaugural Golden couple split just two months later.)

“As far as televised or just like a little private thing, [we] originally thought private—just our friends and family, smaller, maybe destination,” Vassos told E! News in 2024. “But now, we’re thinking maybe we’d be open for a televised one.”

Still, the former school administrator expressed hesitation.

“Bachelor Nation has kind of watched us through this whole journey,” she reasoned, “and then to abruptly end it and not show anybody the next chapter of it would be not really that kind. And so we’re thinking about maybe, maybe doing that.”

At the time, Vassos and Chapple, both 63, expressed hope to walk down the aisle “within a year” but also had more pressing hopes for their then-budding relationship.

“We’re just happy to not have anything epic to do right now because that’s all we’ve been doing for the last six months,” Vassos explained, noting they were looking forward to the opportunity to “be a couple and enjoy each other and get to know each other’s friends and family.”

For the couple, that also meant exploring the possibility of moving to New York together.

“We’re just excited,” Chapple told E! “I think it’s going to be fun to watch Joan handle all this—from the furnishings to everything else to where we live. We get along so well I’m like, 'This is yours. It was my gift to you. You pick it. You pick exactly what you want.’”

In the final episode of The Golden Bachelorette, Chapple had beat out runner-up Guy Gansert in winning Vassos’ heart in his Bora Bora proposal.

"I still think I'm in a dream and I'm just going to wake up,” Chapple told Vassos in the finale. “But Joan, I love you and I want you to be in my life.”

Vassos, for her part, had eagerly accepted Chapple’s hand.

“You made me feel comfortable, you made me feel safe, I felt like we could take care of each other,” she told him, “and I knew you would never hurt me.”

Vassos and Chapple aren’t the only Bachelor Nation couple whose love has wilted. Keep reading for a status update on all the pairs…

Even before Mel Owen's season of The Golden Bachelor kicked off, he was hit with a few penalties after going on the MGoBlue Podcasts With Jon Jansen and insisting, "if they’re 60 or over, I’m cutting them," adding that he wanted them to "try to stay away from the artificial hips and the wigs."

And while Peg Munson is a fellow sexagenarian, his connection with the retired firefighter and bomb tech was quite explosive, with the former NFL linebacker praising her as "high-energy" to Glamour.

Though he didn't take a knee during his November 2025 finale, the sports lawyer still offered up a Neil Lane sparkler, saying, "This represents our commitment to love, to give us time to figure out together what our future holds."

Munson announced in October 2026 that the couple had split, sharing on Instagram, "I did have to make the difficult decision to step away from the relationship. Sometimes, even when there's love, it just doesn't turn out the way that we hoped. So right now, I'm just, I'm just focusing on healing my heart and just focusing on myself, my health, and looking forward to the next chapter.”

According to Spencer Conley (who intrigued Jenn Tran with his "golden retriever energy" on The Bachelorette), he fetched quite the catch on the 10th season of Bachelor in Paradise. Posting a snippet of himself and Jess Edwards (she took a swing at Bachelor Joey Graziadei's heart) revealing that they were falling for one another—Bachelor code for I'm not quite ready to say, "I love you"—he wrote on his Instagram Stories in August 2025 that he'd "already won."

"Everything that we’ve been through," Jess said, "has led us to this exact moment."

Since then, their love led her to Dallas, Jess revealing during Mel's November 2025 After the Finale Rose special that she was Texas-bound in a few weeks.

"This was my best first date," Bailey Taylor Brown wrote on Instagram in August 2025 after she took Jeremy Simon out on Paradise's 10th season. And perhaps her last? Manchester United Carlos Baleba is not expected to return until December at the earliest after suffering a hamstring injury.

The summer signing was forced off early in the second-half of Tuesday’s Champions League game against Roma.

The former Brighton star made a bursting run into the box but pulled up just as he was about to pull the trigger on goal.

Baleba appeared to be in tears as he limped off the Old Trafford pitch. Having already missed the opening five games of the campaign as a result of an ankle injury he suffered during pre-season with Brighton, he now faces another spell on the sidelines.

They are on the road again three days later in a crucial Champions League game against Sporting CP before seeing out the month at home to Newcastle.

Kenyan doomsday cult leader given life sentence for murdering 191 people

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Paul Mackenzie's followers believed he was "Jesus's mouthpiece", the judge said last month

A Kenyan court has sentenced the leader of a doomsday cult, Paul Mackenzie, to 200 years in prison for murdering 191 people, all but one of them children.

Alongside 28 others, Mackenzie was found guilty of the murders for instructing members of his cult to starve their children to death so they could "meet Jesus".

Justice Diana Mochache said Mackenzie bore the "primary criminal culpability" for ordering his followers to fast to death, in one of the world's deadliest episodes of cult activity.

"The sentence is appropriate and just, to send an unmistakable message that such atrocities warrant the maximum penalties allowed by law," the judge added.

The self-styled pastor was arrested in 2023 after the discovery of more than 400 bodies in shallow graves in Shakahola forest near the Kenyan coast. The sprawling investigation into the cases shocked Kenya and drew international attention.

The court sentenced Mackenzie to 100 years in prison for homicide and another 100 years for offences related to starvation.

The sentences within each category will run concurrently, but the two sets will run consecutively, bringing his total prison term to 200 years.

Mackenzie also faces separate manslaughter and terrorism charges, which he denies.

Prosecutors had sought the death penalty, arguing that the scale and circumstances of the killings warranted the harshest punishment.

However, Justice Mochache declined to impose the death penalty, despite ruling that the case met the threshold for its consideration.

"Nevertheless, the court declines the invitation to impose the death penalty on any of the victims," she said, adding that capital punishment would eliminate any prospect of rehabilitation and disengagement. The death sentence has not been carried out in Kenya since 1987.

Mackenzie was tried alongside 28 accomplices, including guards who enforced his fasting orders and gravediggers who helped conceal the victims' bodies in mass graves.

They received varying prison sentences of up to 160 years.

Justice Mochache described the killings as "cold and calculated", saying the perpetrators had shown no remorse and had even declared their willingness to fast to death themselves.

Before the hearing began on Friday morning, Francis Wanje, who lost eight relatives including two grandchildren in Shakahola forest, told the BBC's Newsday programme that he was "hoping for... a sentence which will match his [Mackenzie's] deeds in the forest".

Mackenzie and the co-accused had asked for leniency from the court.

Before their conviction on 19 October, they had all pleaded not guilty and repeatedly insisted that nobody was forced to die.

More than 400 bodies were dug up in Shakahola forest

Mackenzie's followers believed he was "Jesus's mouthpiece", Justice Mochache said as she delivered her eight-hour judgement last month.

Those who opposed Mackenzie's teachings were beaten to death.

One of his co-accused said he had told his followers that anyone who "resisted the directive to fast must be forced to go to heaven through kicks and blows".

Burials inside the forest where the cult was based were called "weddings" and those who died "were celebrated as having wedded with Jesus" the judge said.

The judge found that the deaths arose from an organised system, including religious teachings and directives that compelled followers to follow the deadly fasting.

Post-mortem results showed the cause of deaths to be starvation for most victims, while others died from suffocation, strangulation or head injuries.

The cause of death could not be determined for some, with their bodies having decomposed, while others died soon after their rescue as they were so emaciated.

The Shakahola deaths have shone a spotlight on the lack of oversight of rogue religious activities and how they could have carried on for so long.

The gravity of the case prompted President William Ruto to set up a commission of inquiry, although this was later declared unconstitutional.

Investigations by the state-funded Kenya National Commission on Human Rights (KNCHR) revealed that local administration and law enforcement officials had repeatedly turned a blind eye to the cult's activities.

"We expected at that time the police would take action immediately to go and rescue the other members there, but it didn't happen," said Wanje, who reported the case to the police in March 2023.

Wanje said a relative of his in the police force and a former cult participant had managed to save his grandson at the time. The child told the police what had happened and showed them the grave of his siblings.

"It was [supposed to be] his last day.. they [would have killed] my grandson."

The cult leader had previously been arrested and later released for lack of evidence.

Mackenzie established Good News International Ministries in 2003.

Over the next 16 years, the church grew in popularity, drawing thousands of followers in its branches across Kenya.

The church ran a television station called Times TV which would broadcast his sermons from his church in Malindi.

His End Days sermons included conspiracy theories that described education as "evil", healthcare as "against the doctrine" and digital identity cards as "the mark of the devil".

The main church shut down in 2019 and Mackenzie moved to Shakahola forest, encouraging his devotees to follow him.

While the conclusion of the murder cases may bring a sense of justice for the victims and their families, the convictions only account for 191 victims, while more than 400 bodies were exhumed from the forest.

Meta buys Conductor, whose app runs AI coding agents in parallel

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The startup’s team joins Meta Superintelligence Labs, chief executive Charlie Holtz said. Conductor Cloud shuts down this month, and its desktop app gets updates only until early 2027.

Meta has bought Conductor, a startup whose app lets software engineers run several AI coding agents at once. Its team will join Meta Superintelligence Labs, chief executive Charlie Holtz said. Axios first reported the deal on Wednesday, and Meta did not disclose the price.

Holtz confirmed the deal on X and in a post on Conductor’s site.

“We have some big, crazy plans for the next interface. If you liked Conductor, you’re gonna love what’s coming,” Holtz wrote on X.

He said the team would work with Nat Friedman and Alexandr Wang at the lab, and that it would have “something new” for users early next year. Meta confirmed the deal to TechCrunch but declined to comment further.

Conductor launched its app in July 2025. It runs coding agents from labs such as Anthropic and OpenAI in parallel, each working in its own copy of a project. Holtz and chief technology officer Jackson de Campos founded the company in 2024, Axios reported.

The desktop app will keep getting new models and critical bug fixes until early 2027, Conductor said.

The deal came the same day OpenAI said it would buy Pierre Computer Company, which makes tools for storing and managing code. Both deals target developers, whom Axios called the most important power users of AI. They compete with products such as Anthropic’s Claude Code and OpenAI’s Codex.

Meta launched its own coding agent, Muse Code, in August. Its Muse assistant has passed 10 million users. Its $2bn purchase of agent startup Manus fell through after China blocked the deal.

How Mid-Sized Businesses Can Thrive In Economic Uncertainty

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Nik Froehlich is the CEO and Founder of Saritasa, an Information Technology and Services company, since 2005.

When the economy tightens, most mid-sized companies respond the same way. They cut travel, headcount, projects and tools. Cutting feels decisive, and in the short term, it often works.​

I have watched this pattern for a long time; first while scaling a concrete business across multiple states, and then over 20 years building a custom software development firm. Cost cutting is sometimes necessary. When it becomes the primary strategy, though, it usually delays the real problem. You end up with a thinner company still running the same broken processes.​

The companies that come out of a downturn stronger use the pressure differently by fixing how the work actually gets done.​

Inflation peaked at 9.1% in June 2022, the highest level in four decades, and the federal funds rate climbed from near zero to above 4% within a year. Capital became expensive and operating costs rose. Four years later, the pressure has not eased: Consumer prices were still up 3.4% year over year in August 2026, well above the Federal Reserve’s 2% target. Research across three recessions from Harvard Business School found that the companies most likely to outperform after a downturn cut costs mainly by improving operational efficiency rather than through blanket reductions.​

When I mention “efficiency software,” I mean systems that remove friction from the actual work: the rework, the duplicate data entry, the workarounds people build when a process breaks. The gains are rarely glamorous, and they show up across very different industries.​

Wingstop, a fast-casual restaurant chain, invested in digital ordering and workflow software and now processes more than 60% of sales through digital channels. That shift contributed to a 25% increase in same-store sales over two years while easing labor pressure in its restaurants.

Texmark Chemicals, a mid-sized Texas processor, deployed industrial IoT and predictive maintenance software across its plant. The company projects a 50% reduction in planned maintenance costs and expects to recover roughly 1,000 labor hours a year previously spent on manual equipment inspections.

In retail, Sam’s Club piloted an AI-based demand forecasting tool that improved accuracy enough to roll out across every club. Mid-sized retailers have since adopted the same approach at a smaller scale to reduce excess inventory and stockouts.​

The common thread in these cases has little to do with the technology itself. In each one, someone took ownership of a specific operational problem and built a system that made the work better.​

Before approving any efficiency initiative, I apply a two-part test. Someone must own the outcome once the system is live, and the system must still be usable and affordable three years out.

Most projects fail one or both parts. They get built, they look good in the demo and then they slowly become someone else’s problem. Custom software creates lasting value when it is designed around clear ownership and long-term maintainability.​

One way to find a good candidate is to watch where the hours go. I ask teams to list the tasks that take up the most time each week, and then sort that time into two groups: hours spent doing the work itself and hours spent clearing the way for it. Manual, repetitive tasks usually take the bulk of the time. I look for areas where a small fix can result in an outsized impact. For example, better tracking of hours and materials in the field can speed up billing and cut down on invoice disputes, even though the change itself is small. These kinds of changes rarely make the list of transformation projects. ​

The strongest candidates automate manual processes, improve workflow visibility, strengthen customer service and retention or tighten inventory and supply chain operations. Research from McKinsey & Company suggests digital collaboration tools alone can yield efficiency gains of 20% to 30%. ​

I also use a “subtraction test” to differentiate between a real, measurable improvement and one that moves cost somewhere else. A worthwhile initiative retires something; when a new tool adds a system without taking anything away, someone still has to learn and maintain it, so the friction usually ends up in a different department. Before I approve anything new, I want the team to name what goes away.​

Measuring impact requires choosing one number before the work begins, such as hours spent on a task or days to send an invoice, and recording where it stands. Then, I check that number shortly after launch and again several months later, since early gains often fade once people drift back to old workarounds or the person who championed the project moves on. An improvement that holds up at the second check has usually become part of how the team works, and that is the clearest sign it will pass the three-year test.​

In uncertain times, pure cost cutting feels safe. It is also incomplete. A company can only cut so far before it damages its ability to deliver. The better path pairs disciplined cost control with targeted investments that make the remaining work more productive. That is harder than issuing a mandate. It requires an honest look at how the work gets done and where the friction lives.​

After twenty years of watching mid-sized businesses try both approaches, I keep arriving at the same conclusion. The companies that treat efficiency as an ownership problem come out of the cycle stronger than the ones that simply got smaller.​​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?
